# a unified single-loop alternating gradient projection algorithm for nonconvex-concave and convex-nonconcave minimax problems

zi xu  $\cdot$  huiling zhang  $\cdot$  yang xu  $\cdot$  guanghui lan

received: date / accepted: date

abstract much recent research effort has been directed to the development of efficient algorithms for solving minimax problems with theoretical convergence guarantees due to the relevance of these problems to a few emergent applications. in this paper, we propose a unified single-loop alternating gradient projection (agp) algorithm for solving smooth nonconvex-(strongly) concave and (strongly) convex-nonconcave minimax problems. agp employs simple gradient projection steps for updating the primal and dual variables alternatively at each iteration. we show that it can find an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of the objective function in  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\right)$  (resp.  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-4}\right)$ ) iterations under nonconvex-strongly concave (resp. nonconvex-concave) setting. moreover, its gradient complexity to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of the objective function is bounded by  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\right)$  (resp.,  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-4}\right)$ ) under the strongly convex-nonconcave (resp., convex-nonconcave) setting. to the best of our knowledge, this is the first time that a simple and unified single-loop algorithm is developed for solving both nonconvex-(strongly) concave and (strongly) convex-nonconcave minimax problems. moreover, the complexity results for solving the latter (strongly) convex-nonconcave minimax problems have never been obtained before in the literature. numerical results show the efficiency of the proposed agp algorithm.

furthermore, we extend the agp algorithm by presenting a block alternating proximal gradient (bapg) algorithm for solving more general multi-block nonsmooth nonconvex-(strongly) concave and (strongly) convex-nonconcave minimax problems. we can similarly establish the gradient complexity of the proposed algorithm under these four different settings.

keywords minimax optimization problem  $\cdot$  alternating gradient projection algorithm  $\cdot$  iteration complexity  $\cdot$  single-loop algorithm  $\cdot$  machine learning

mathematics subject classification (2020) msc 90c47 · msc 90c26 · 90c30

# 1 introduction

we consider the following minimax optimization problem:

$$
\min  _ {x \in \mathcal {x}} \max  _ {y \in \mathcal {y}} f (x, y), \tag {p}
$$

where  $\mathcal{x} \subseteq \mathbb{r}^n$  and  $\mathcal{y} \subseteq \mathbb{r}^m$  are nonempty closed and bounded convex sets, and  $f: \mathcal{x} \times \mathcal{y} \to \mathbb{r}$  is a smooth function. this problem has attracted more attention due to its wide applications in machine learning, signal processing, and many other research fields in recent years. many practical problems can be formulated as in (p), such as the power control and transceiver design problem in signal processing [33], distributed nonconvex optimization [34,29,16], robust learning over multiple domains [46], statistical learning [1,19] and many others.

minimax optimization problems have been studied for many years, but most previous works focused on convex-concave minimax problems, i.e.,  $f(x,y)$  is convex with respect to  $x$  and concave with respect to  $y$  [38, 7, 9]. under this setting, nemirovski [39] proposed a mirror-prox algorithm which returns an  $\varepsilon$ -saddle point within the complexity of  $\mathcal{o}(1 / \varepsilon)$  when  $\mathcal{x}$  and  $\mathcal{y}$  are bounded sets. nesterov [41] developed a dual extrapolation algorithm which owns the same complexity bound as in [39]. monteiro and svaiter [36, 37] extended the complexity result to unbounded sets and composite objectives by using the hybrid proximal extragradient algorithm with a different termination criterion. tseng [50] proved the same result using a refined convergence analysis. abernethy et al. [2] presented a hamiltonian gradient descent algorithm with last-iterate convergence under a "sufficient bilinear" condition. a few other papers have studied special cases in the convex-concave setting, for more details, we refer to [9, 10, 13, 20, 27, 32, 44, 45] and the references therein.

however, recent applications of problem (p) in machine learning and signal processing urge the necessity of moving beyond this classical setting. for example, in a typical generative adversary network (gans) problem formulation [48], the objective function  $f(x,y)$  is nonconvex with respect to (w.r.t.)  $x$  and concave w.r.t.  $y$ . as another example, in a robust support vector machine problem, we have  $y \equiv (y_{u},y_{v}) \in \mathbb{r}^{m} \times \mathbb{r}$ ,  $x \equiv (x_w,x_b) \in \mathbb{r}^m \times \mathbb{r}$  and the objective function  $f(x,y) = y_v[\langle x_w,y_u\rangle +x_b]$ . this problem is convex w.r.t.  $x$ , but not necessarily concave w.r.t.  $y$ .

most of recent studies focus on nonconvex-(strongly) concave minimax problems. for nonconvex-strongly concave minimax problem, several recent works [24, 47, 31, 33] have studied various algorithms, and all of them can achieve the gradient complexity of  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\kappa_y^2\varepsilon^{-2}\right)$  in terms of stationary point of  $\varphi(\cdot)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{y}}f(\cdot,y)$  (when  $\mathcal{x}=\mathbb{r}^n$ ), or stationary point of  $f$ , where  $\kappa_y$  is the condition number for  $f(x,\cdot)$ . lin et al.[32] propose an accelerated algorithm which can improve the gradient complexity bound to  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\sqrt{\kappa_y\varepsilon^{-2}}\right)$ .

for general nonconvex-concave minimax problem, there are two types of algorithms, i.e., nested-loop algorithms and single-loop algorithms. one intensively studied type is nested-loop algorithms. rafique et al. [47] propose a proximally guided stochastic mirror descent method (pg-smd/pgsvrg), which updates  $x$  and  $y$  simultaneously, and provably converges to an approximate stationary point of  $\varphi(\cdot)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{y}}f(\cdot,y)$ . however, only partial convergence results were established for nonconvex-linear minimax problem. nouriehed et al. [43] propose an alternative multi-step framework that finds an  $\varepsilon$ -first order nash equilibrium of  $f$  with  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}(\varepsilon^{-3.5})$  gradient evaluations. very recently, thekumparampil et al. [49] propose a proximal dual implicit accelerated gradient algorithm and proved that the algorithm finds an approximate stationary point of  $\varphi(\cdot)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{y}}f(\cdot,y)$  with the rate of  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}(\varepsilon^{-3})$ . under an equivalent notion of stationary point, kong and monteiro [25] propose an accelerated inexact proximal point smoothing method to achieve the same rate, however, at each outer iteration of their algorithm, a perturbed smooth approximation of the inner maximization subproblem needs to be solved, and the complexity of solving the inner problem has not been considered in [25]. lin et al. [32] propose a class of accelerated algorithms for smooth nonconvex-concave minimax problems, which achieves a gradient complexity bound of  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}(\varepsilon^{-2.5})$  in terms of stationary point of  $f$ . all these nested-loop algorithms either employ multiple gradient ascent steps for  $y$ 's update to solve the inner subproblem exactly or inexactly, or further do similar acceleration for  $x$ 's update by adding regularization terms to the inner objective function, and thus are relatively complicated to be implemented.

on the other hand, fewer studies have been directed to single-loop algorithms for nonconvex-concave minimax problems, even though these methods are more popular in practice due to their simplicity. one such method is the gradient descent-ascent (gda) method, which performs a gradient descent step on  $x$  and a gradient ascent step on  $y$  simultaneously at each iteration. however, this algorithm fails to converge even for simple bilinear zero-sum games [28]. many improved gda algorithms have been proposed in [8,11,12,17,18,22]. however, the theoretical understanding of gda is fairly limited. very recently, by setting the stepsize of updating  $x$  in the order of  $\varepsilon^4$ , lin et al. [31] proved that the iteration complexity of gda to find an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of  $\varphi(\cdot)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{y}}f(\cdot,y)$  is bounded by  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}(\varepsilon^{-6})$  for nonconvex-concave minimax problems when  $\mathcal{x}=\mathbb{r}^{n}$  and  $\mathcal{v}$  is a

convex compact set. if both  $x$  and  $y$  are constrained, the complexity of gda still remains unknown. jin et al. [24] propose a gdmax algorithm with iteration complexity  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\varepsilon^{-6}\right)$ , which corresponds to the number of times the inner maximization problem is solved. moreover, lu et al. [33] proposed another single-loop algorithm for nonconvex minimax problems, namely the hybrid block successive approximation (hibsa) algorithm, which can obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of  $f(x,y)$  in  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}\left(\varepsilon^{-4}\right)$  iterations when  $\mathcal{x}$  and  $\mathcal{y}$  are convex compact sets. at each iteration, for updating  $x$ , one has to solve the subproblem of minimizing a strongly convex function majorizing the original function  $f$  for a fixed  $y$ , while for updating  $y$  one has to solve the subproblem of maximizing the original function  $f(x,y)$  plus some regularization terms for a fixed  $x$ . under the nonconvex-concave (not strongly concave) setting, both complexity results in [33] and [24] count the number of times the inner maximization problem is solved, without taking into account the complexity of solving the inner maximization problem. moreover, different stopping rules have been adopted in these existing works, e.g., [32, 33, 43].

as mentioned earlier, another interesting class of minimax problems is the (strongly) convex-nonconcave setting of (p), i.e.,  $f(x,y)$  is (strongly) convex w.r.t.  $x$  and nonconcave w.r.t.  $y$ . however, for any given  $x$ , to solve the inner maximization subproblem, i.e.,  $\max_{y\in \mathcal{v}}f(x,y)$ , is already np-hard. due to this reason, almost all the existing nested-loop algorithms will lose their theoretical guarantees since they need to solve the inner subproblem exactly, or approximately with an error proportional to the accuracy  $\varepsilon$ . most existing single-loop algorithms, e.g., hibsa or gdmax, will also get stuck, since they require the solution of the inner maximization problem. one possible alternative approach would be to switch the order of the "min" and "max" operators. however, in general,  $\min_{x\in \mathcal{x}}\max_{y\in \mathcal{y}}f(x,y)\neq \max_{y\in \mathcal{y}}\min_{x\in \mathcal{x}}f(x,y)$  when  $f(x,y)$  is nonconvex w.r.t.  $x$  or nonconcave w.r.t.  $y$ . the set of stationary points for these problems obtained by switching the order of "min" and "max" could also be different under some criterions, e.g.,  $\| \nabla \phi (\cdot)\| \leq \varepsilon$  with  $\varphi(\cdot)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{v}}f(\cdot,y)$  which is meaningful when  $f(\cdot,y)$  is strongly concave with respect to  $y$  as defined in [32]. whereas the set of stationary points for the above two problems might the same (e.g., in terms of the stationarity of  $f$ ), the algorithms applied to these problems may have drastically different trajectories and would converge to quite different solutions.

it is worth mentioning the most general nonconvex-nonconcave minimax problems, i.e.,  $f(x,y)$  is nonconvex w.r.t.  $x$  and nonconcave w.r.t  $y$ . it should be noted that it is unclear whether a stationary point exists or not for solving these general minimax problems. most recent works aimed at defining a notion of goodness or developing new practical algorithms for reducing oscillations and speeding up the convergence of gradient dynamics [3,12, 21,23,35]. the convergence results for all these algorithms hold either in local region or asymptotically and hence can not imply the global convergence rate. recently, flokas et al. [15] analyze the gda algorithm for nonconvex-nonconcave zero-sum games, i.e.,  $\mathcal{x} = \mathbb{r}^n$  and  $\mathcal{y} = \mathbb{r}^m$ . lin et al. [30] propose a proximal algorithm to solve a special case of the nonconvex-nonconcave minimax problem, where the function  $f(x,y)$  satisfies a generalized monotone variational inequality condition (see [14]), and show its convergence to stationary points. nouriehed et al. [43] study a special class of nonconvex-nonconcave minimax problems, in which  $f(x,\cdot)$  satisfies the polyak-lojasiewic(pl) condition. more recently, yang et al. [51] study the two-sided pl minimax problems and proposed a variance reduced strategy for solving them.

contributions. in this paper, we focus on single-loop algorithms for solving nonconvex-concave and convex-nonconcave minimax problems. our main contributions are as follows.

we propose a simple and unified single-loop alternating gradient projection (agp) algorithm for solving both nonconvex-(strongly) concave and (strongly) convex-nonconcave smooth minimax problems. at each iteration, only simple gradient projection steps are employed for updating  $x$  and  $y$  alternatively.

we analyze the gradient complexity of the proposed unified agp algorithm under four different settings. for the nonconvex-concave setting, we show that an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of  $f$  can be obtained in  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\right)$  (resp.  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-4}\right)$ ) iterations for nonconvex-strongly concave (resp. nonconvex concave) minimax problems. to the best of our knowledge, these represent the state-of-the-art single loop algorithms under nonconvex-concave settings. secondly, we show that the gradient complexity to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of  $f$  is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\right)$  (resp.,  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-4}\right)$ ) under the strongly convex-nonconcave (resp., convex-nonconcave) setting. to the best of our knowledge, these are the first two theoretically guaranteed convergence results reported in the literature under these settings. existing single-loop algorithms under both nonconvex-concave and convex-nonconcave settings are summarized in table 1.

as shown in table 1, agp matches the best-known  $o(\epsilon^{-2})$  complexity for the nonconvex-strongly concave setting, whereas for the general nonconvex-concave setting, it improves the best-known complexity for single-loop algorithms from  $o(\epsilon^{-6})$  to  $o(\epsilon^{-4})$ . a key step in our development is to construct a suitable potential function which involves function value plus some distance between two adjacent iterates for the nonconvex-strongly concave and strongly convex-nonconcave case, whereas an additional quadratic regularization term of  $x$  or  $y$  is needed for the general nonconvex-concave and convex-nonconcave case (see lemma 3.4 and lemma

4.4). to the best of our knowledge, this is the first time that such potential functions have been constructed for solving minimax problems. moreover, the functional descent results in lemmas 3.1, 3.3, 4.1, and 4.3 have not been reported before and appear to be novel from our point of view. it should be noted that the complexity of agp does not match the best-known  $o(\epsilon^{-2.5})$  complexity possessed by nested-loop algorithms for the general nonconvex-concave case. one possible reason is that only one gradient projection step is employed for solving the inner problem in single loop algorithms, and hence the error associated with the gradient for the outer problem will be larger than that for nested loop algorithms and such errors will accumulate as the algorithms proceeds. this also explains why their iteration complexity analysis might be more difficult than that of nested-looped algorithms. it is not evident to us whether or not this complexity bound obtained for single-loop algorithms can be further improved. nevertheless, due to its simplicity and the fact that it does not require the input and fine-tuning of too many algorithmic parameters, the proposed agp algorithm can numerically outperform the state-of-the-art nested loop algorithms as shown in section 6.

furthermore, agp provides a flexible algorithmic framework that can be easily generalized to solve more complicated minimax problems. based on the basic idea of the agp algorithm, we propose a block alternating proximal gradient (bapg) algorithm for solving more general nonsmooth multi-block nonconvex-(strongly) concave and (strongly) convex-nonconcave minimax problems. each bapg iteration requires only simple proximal gradient steps to update each block of the multi-block variables alternatively. we prove the gradient complexity of the proposed bapg algorithm under these four different settings. to the best of our knowledge, existing algorithms (especially the ones with nested loop) can hardly be extended to these more complicated multi-block settings and these complexity results have not been obtained before.

table 1 single-loop algorithms for solving (p) in one block smooth nonconvex-concave and convex-nonconcave settings

this complexity is to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of  $\varphi(\cdot) = \max_{y \in \mathcal{y}} f(\cdot, y)$ , i.e.,  $\|\nabla \varphi(\cdot)\| \leq \varepsilon$  when  $\mathcal{x} = \mathbb{r}^n$  and  $\mathcal{y}$  is a convex compact set.  
2 this complexity is to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of  $f$  which is defined as in section 2, when  $\mathcal{x}$  and  $\mathcal{y}$  are both convex compact sets.  
this complexity corresponds to the number of times the inner maximization problem is solved. thus it does not consider the complexity of solving the inner problem.

the rest of this paper is organized as follows. in section 2, we propose a unified alternating gradient projection (agp) algorithm for nonconvex-(strongly) concave and (strongly) convex-concave minimax problems, and we then analyze the corresponding gradient complexity for four different settings in section 3 and section 4. we propose a block alternating proximal gradient (bapg) algorithm for solving more general nonsmooth multiblock nonconvex-(strongly) concave and (strongly) convex-nonconcave minimax problems, and also establish the corresponding gradient complexity for four different settings in section 5. we report some numerical results in section 6 and make some concluding remarks in the last section.

notation. for vectors, we use  $\| \cdot \|$  to denote the  $l_{2}$ -norm. for a function  $f(x,y): \mathbb{r}^n \times \mathbb{r}^m \to \mathbb{r}$ , we use  $\nabla_x f(x,y)$  (or  $\nabla_y f(x,y)$ ) to denote the partial gradient of  $f$  with respect to the first variable (or the second variable) at point  $(x,y)$ . let  $\mathcal{p}_{\mathcal{x}}$  and  $\mathcal{p}_{\mathcal{y}}$  denote projections onto the sets  $\mathcal{x}$  and  $\mathcal{y}$ . finally, we use the notation  $\mathcal{o}(\cdot)$  to hide only absolute constants which do not depend on any problem parameter, and  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}(\cdot)$  notation to hide only absolute constants and log factors.

a continuously differentiable function  $f(\cdot)$  is called  $\theta$ -strongly convex if there exists a constant  $\theta > 0$  such that for any  $x, y \in \mathcal{x}$ ,

$$
f (y) \geq f (x) + \langle \nabla f (x), y - x \rangle + \frac {\theta}{2} \| y - x \| ^ {2}. \tag {1.1}
$$

if  $-f$  satisfies (1.1),  $f(\cdot)$  is called  $\theta$ -strongly concave. a pair  $(x^{*},y^{*})$  is a nash equilibrium (or equivalently a saddle point) of function  $f$ , if  $\forall x\in \mathcal{x}$ ,  $\forall y\in \mathcal{v}$ ,

$$
f \left(x ^ {*}, y\right) \leq f \left(x ^ {*}, y ^ {*}\right) \leq f \left(x, y ^ {*}\right). \tag {1.2}
$$

a pair  $(x^{*},y^{*})$  is a local nash equilibrium (or equivalently a local saddle point) of  $f$ , if there exists  $\delta >0$  such that for any  $(x,y)$  in  $\mathcal{x}\times \mathcal{y}$  and  $\| x - x^{*}\| \leq \delta$ ,  $\| y - y^{*}\| \leq \delta$ , (1.2) is satisfied. a pair  $(x^{*},y^{*})$  is an  $\varepsilon$ -first-order

nash equilibrium of function  $f$ , if  $\mathcal{x}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq \varepsilon$  and  $\mathcal{y}(x^{*},y^{*})\leq \varepsilon$ , where

$$
\mathcal {x} \left(x ^ {*}, y ^ {*}\right) := - \min  _ {x} \langle \nabla_ {x} f \left(x ^ {*}, y ^ {*}\right), x - x ^ {*} \rangle \quad s. t. x \in \mathcal {x}, \| x - x ^ {*} \| \leq 1,
$$

$$
\mathcal {y} (x ^ {*}, y ^ {*}) := \max  _ {y} \langle \nabla_ {y} f (x ^ {*}, y ^ {*}), y - y ^ {*} \rangle \quad s. t. y \in \mathcal {y}, \| y - y ^ {*} \| \leq 1.
$$

# 2 an alternating gradient projection algorithm for (p)

in this section, we propose a unified alternating gradient projection (agp) algorithm that will be used later for solving a few different classes of (p). each iteration of the proposed agp algorithm consists of two gradient projection steps for updating both  $x$  and  $y$ . instead of the original function  $f(x,y)$ , at the  $k$ -th iteration, agp uses the gradient of a regularized version of the original function, i.e.,

$$
f _ {k} (x, y) = f (x, y) + \frac {b _ {k}}{2} \| x \| ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {k}}{2} \| y \| ^ {2}, \tag {2.1}
$$

where  $b_{k} \geq 0$  and  $c_{k} \geq 0$  are two regularization parameters. more specifically, for a given pair  $(x_{k}, y_{k}) \in \mathcal{x} \times \mathcal{y}$ , agp minimizes a linearized approximation of  $f_{k}(x, y_{k})$  plus some regularized terms to update  $x_{k}$  as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} x _ {k + 1} = \arg \min  _ {x \in \mathcal {x}} \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right), x - x _ {k} \right\rangle + \frac {\beta_ {k}}{2} \| x - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ = \mathcal {p} _ {\chi} \left(x _ {k} - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k}} \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k}} b _ {k} x _ {k}\right), \tag {2.2} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\mathcal{p}_{\mathcal{x}}$  is the projection operator onto  $\mathcal{x}$  and  $\beta_{k} > 0$  denotes a stepsize parameter. similarly, it updates  $y_{k}$  by maximizing a linearized approximation of  $f_{k}(x_{k + 1},y)$  minus some regularized terms, i.e.,

$$
\begin{array}{l} y _ {k + 1} = \arg \max  _ {y \in \mathcal {y}} \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k} \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right), y - y _ {k} \right\rangle - \frac {\gamma_ {k}}{2} \| y - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ = \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {y}} \left(y _ {k} + \frac {1}{\gamma_ {k}} \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \frac {1}{\gamma_ {k}} c _ {k} y _ {k}\right), \tag {2.3} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\mathcal{p}_{\mathcal{y}}$  is the projection operator onto  $\mathcal{y}$  and  $\gamma_{k} > 0$  is another stepsize parameter. the proposed agp method is formally stated in algorithm 1, where sequences  $\{\beta_k\}, \{b_k\}, \{\gamma_k\}, \{c_k\}$  and the stopping rule in step 4 will be specified later in each of the different problem settings to be studied.

# algorithm 1 (an alternating gradient projection (agp) algorithm)

step 1: input  $x_{1}, y_{1}, \beta_{1}, \gamma_{1}, b_{1}, c_{1}$ ; set  $k = 1$ .

step 2: calculate  $\beta_{k}$  and  $b_{k}$ , and perform the following update for  $x_{k}$ :

$$
x _ {k + 1} = \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {x}} \left(x _ {k} - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k}} \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k}} b _ {k} x _ {k}\right). \tag {2.4}
$$

step 3: calculate  $\gamma_{k}$  and  $c_{k}$ , and perform the following update for  $y_{k}$ :

$$
y _ {k + 1} = \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {y}} \left(y _ {k} + \frac {1}{\gamma_ {k}} \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \frac {1}{\gamma_ {k}} c _ {k} y _ {k}\right). \tag {2.5}
$$

step 4: if some stationary condition is satisfied, stop; otherwise, set  $k = k + 1$ , go to step 2.

observe that if we set  $b_{k} = 0$  and  $c_{k} = 0$ , the agp algorithm is exactly the alternating version of the gda algorithm, which is rather natural and has been widely used by practitioners for solving minimax problems, e.g., in generative adversarial networks. however, to the best of our knowledge, even for the alternating gda algorithm, the convergence for solving (p) has never been established before in the literature. moreover, if  $b_{k}$  or  $c_{k}$  is not equal to 0, agp algorithm is completely new. it turns out that  $b_{k}$  or  $c_{k}$  plays a very crucial role to guarantee the convergence of agp when solving general nonconvex-concave or convex-nonconcave minimax problems.

before we prove the iteration complexity of agp algorithm for solving (p), we define the stationarity gap as the termination criterion as follows.

definition 2.1 at each iteration of algorithm 1, the stationarity gap for problem (p) w.r.t.  $f(x,y)$  is defined as:

$$
\nabla g \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) := \left[ \begin{array}{c} \beta_ {k} \left(x _ {k} - \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {x}} \big (x _ {k} - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k}} \nabla_ {x} f \big (x _ {k}, y _ {k} \big) \big)\right) \\ \gamma_ {k} \left(y _ {k} - \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {y}} \big (y _ {k} + \frac {1}{\gamma_ {k}} \nabla_ {y} f \big (x _ {k}, y _ {k} \big) \big)\right) \end{array} \right].
$$

we denote  $\nabla g_k \coloneqq \nabla g(x_k, y_k)$ ,  $(\nabla g_k)_x \coloneqq \beta_k(x_k - \mathcal{p}_{\mathcal{x}}(x_k - \frac{1}{\beta_k} \nabla_x f(x_k, y_k)))$ , and  $(\nabla g_k)_y \coloneqq \gamma_k(y_k - \mathcal{p}_{\mathcal{y}}(y_k + \frac{1}{\gamma_k} \nabla_y f(x_k, y_k)))$ .

note that the widely used metrics for convex-concave minimax problems such as the min-max value or the distance to the set of optimal solutions of the min-max problem are not applicable in nonconvex cases. for the latter cases we call  $(x_{k},y_{k})$  an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of  $f(x,y)$  if  $\| \nabla g(x_k,y_k)\| \leq \varepsilon$  with  $\nabla g(x_k,y_k)$  being defined as in definition 2.1, which has been widely used as the optimality measure, e.g., [32,33]. in the absence of constraints,  $\| \nabla g(x_k,y_k)\| \leq \varepsilon$  reduces to the standard condition  $\| \nabla_x f(x_k,y_k)\| \leq \varepsilon$  and  $\| \nabla_y f(x_k,y_k)\| \leq \varepsilon$  for unconstrained problems. the vector  $\nabla g(x_k,y_k)$  also refers to gradient mapping at  $(x_{k},y_{k})$ , see [42] for the details.

definition 2.2 at each iteration of algorithm 1, the stationarity gap for problem (p) w.r.t.  $f_{k}(x,y)$  is defined as:

$$
\nabla \tilde {g} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) := \left[ \begin{array}{c} \beta_ {k} \left(x _ {k} - \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {x}} (x _ {k} - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k}} \nabla_ {x} f _ {k} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}))\right) \\ \gamma_ {k} \left(y _ {k} - \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {y}} (y _ {k} + \frac {1}{\gamma_ {k}} \nabla_ {y} f _ {k} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}))\right) \end{array} \right].
$$

we denote  $\nabla \tilde{g}_k \coloneqq \nabla \tilde{g}(x_k, y_k)$ ,  $(\nabla \tilde{g}_k)_x \coloneqq \beta_k(x_k - \mathcal{p}_{\mathcal{x}}(x_k - \frac{1}{\beta_k} \nabla_x f_k(x_k, y_k)))$ , and  $(\nabla \tilde{g}_k)_y \coloneqq \gamma_k(y_k - \mathcal{p}_{\mathcal{y}}(y_k + \frac{1}{\gamma_k} \nabla_y f_k(x_k, y_k)))$ .

we also need to make the following assumption about the smoothness of  $f(x,y)$ .

assumption 2.1  $f(x,y)$  has lipschitz continuous gradients, i.e., there exist positive scalars  $l_{22}, l_{12}, l_{11}, l_{21}$  such that for any  $x,\bar{x} \in \mathcal{x}, y,\bar{y} \in \mathcal{y}$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \nabla_ {x} f (x, y) - \nabla_ {x} f (\bar {x}, y) \right\| \leq l _ {1 1} \| x - \bar {x} \|, \\ \| \nabla_ {x} f (x, y) - \nabla_ {x} f (x, \bar {y}) \| \leq l _ {2 1} \| y - \bar {y} \|, \\ \| \nabla_ {y} f (x, \bar {y}) - \nabla_ {y} f (x, y) \| \leq l _ {2 2} \| \bar {y} - y \|, \\ \| \nabla_ {y} f (x, y) - \nabla_ {y} f (\bar {x}, y) \| \leq l _ {1 2} \| x - \bar {x} \|. \\ \end{array}
$$

we denote  $l\coloneqq \max \{l_{11},l_{22},l_{12},l_{21}\}$

under this assumption, we can first prove the following lemma for estimating bounds on changes in the function value when  $x_{k}$  or  $y_{k}$  is updated at each iteration in algorithm 1.

lemma 2.1 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1. then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \leq - \left(\beta_ {k} - \frac {l _ {1 1}}{2}\right) \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} - \frac {b _ {k}}{2} \left[ \left\| x _ {k + 1} \right\| ^ {2} - \left\| x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} - \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} \right], (2.6) \\ f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) \geq \left(\gamma_ {k} - \frac {l _ {2 2}}{2}\right) \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} + \frac {c _ {k}}{2} \left[ \left\| y _ {k + 1} \right\| ^ {2} - \left\| y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} - \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} \right]. (2.7) \\ \end{array}
$$

proof firstly, by the optimality condition for (2.4), we have

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) + \beta_ {k} \left(x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k}\right) + b _ {k} x _ {k}, x _ {k} - x _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \geq 0. \tag {2.8}
$$

by assumption 2.1, the gradient of  $f$  is lipschitz continuous, implying that

$$
f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \leq \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\rangle + \frac {l _ {1 1}}{2} \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {2.9}
$$

adding (2.8) and (2.9), we can easily show that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \leq - \left(\beta_ {k} - \frac {l _ {1 1}}{2}\right) \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} - b _ {k} \langle x _ {k}, x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \rangle \\ = - \left(\beta_ {k} - \frac {l _ {1 1}}{2}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {b _ {k}}{2} \left[ \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \| x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

this completes the proof of (2.6). by the optimality condition for (2.5), we have

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \gamma_ {k} \left(y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k}\right) - c _ {k} y _ {k}, y _ {k} - y _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \leq 0. \tag {2.10}
$$

by assumption 2.1, the gradient of  $f$  is lipschitz continuous, implying that

$$
f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) \geq \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle - \frac {l _ {2 2}}{2} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {2.11}
$$

the result (2.7) then follows by adding (2.10) and (2.11).

in the following two sections, we will establish the iteration complexity of the agp algorithm under four different problem settings. although there are some different technical details under different problem settings, the main process used in these proofs is similar. firstly, we estimate a bound on the change of function values between two adjacent iterates, shown as in lemmas 3.1, 3.3, 4.1 and 4.3 respectively. then, we estimate an upper bound on the weighted distance between two adjacent pairs of iterates by constructing a suitable potential function according to different properties of the objective function, shown as in lemmas 3.2, 3.4, 4.2 and 4.4 respectively. finally, by using those upper bounds, we prove the complexity of the algorithm through some careful parameter selection, shown as in theorems 3.1, 3.2, 4.1 and 4.2.

# 3 complexity analysis for nonconvex-concave minimax problems

# 3.1 nonconvex-strongly concave setting

in this subsection, we analyze the iteration complexity of algorithm 1 for solving nonconvex-strongly concave minimax optimization problems, i.e.,  $f(x,y)$  is nonconvex w.r.t.  $x$  for any fixed  $y \in \mathcal{v}$ , and  $\mu$ -strongly concave w.r.t.  $y$  for any given  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ . under this setting,  $\forall k \geq 1$ , we set

$$
\beta_ {k} = \eta , \gamma_ {k} = \frac {1}{\rho}, \text {a n d} b _ {k} = c _ {k} = 0 \tag {3.1}
$$

in algorithm 1, and simplify the update for  $x_{k}$  and  $y_{k}$  as follows:

$$
x _ {k + 1} = \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {x}} \left(x _ {k} - \frac {1}{\eta} \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right)\right), \tag {3.2}
$$

$$
y _ {k + 1} = \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {y}} \left(y _ {k} + \rho \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right)\right), \tag {3.3}
$$

which is exactly the alternating version of gda algorithm. our goal in the remaining part of this subsection is to establish the iteration complexity of algorithm 1 under the nonconvex-strongly concave setting.

we now establish an important recursion for the agp algorithm.

lemma 3.1 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (3.1). if  $\eta > l_{11}$ , then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \\ \leq - \left(\frac {\eta}{2} - \frac {l _ {1 2} ^ {2} \rho}{2}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \left(\frac {\mu}{2} - \frac {1}{\rho}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ - \left(\mu - \frac {1}{2 \rho} - \frac {\rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2}\right) \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.4} \\ \end{array}
$$

proof the optimality condition for  $y_{k}$  in (3.3) implies that  $\forall y\in \mathcal{v}$  and  $\forall k\geq 1$

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \frac {1}{\rho} \left(y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k}\right), y - y _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \leq 0. \tag {3.5}
$$

by choosing  $y = y_{k}$  in (3.5), we have

$$
\langle \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - \frac {1}{\rho} (y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k}), y _ {k} - y _ {k + 1} \rangle \leq 0. \tag {3.6}
$$

on the other hand, by replacing  $k$  with  $k - 1$  and choosing  $y = y_{k + 1}$  in (3.5), we obtain

$$
\langle \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \frac {1}{\rho} (y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1}), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle \leq 0, \tag {3.7}
$$

which, in view of the fact that  $f(x,y)$  is  $\mu$ -strongly concave w.r.t.  $y$  for any given  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ , then implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) \\ \leq \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle - \frac {\mu}{2} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} \\ \leq \langle \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle \\ + \frac {1}{\rho} \left\langle y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1}, y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle - \frac {\mu}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.8} \\ \end{array}
$$

denoting  $v_{k + 1} \coloneqq (y_{k + 1} - y_k) - (y_k - y_{k - 1})$ , we can write the first inner product term in the r.h.s. of (3.8) as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle \\ = \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), v _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\rangle . \tag {3.9} \\ \end{array}
$$

next, we estimate the three terms in the right hand side of (3.9) respectively. by assumption 2.1 and the cauchy-schwarz inequality, we can bound the first two terms according to

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle \\ \leq \frac {l _ {1 2} ^ {2} \rho}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \rho} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2}, \tag {3.10} \\ \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), v _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \leq \frac {\rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2} \left\| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \rho} \left\| v _ {k + 1} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {3.11}
$$

for the third term, by  $\mu$ -strongly-concavity of  $f$  with respect to  $y$ ,

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\rangle \leq - \mu \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.12}
$$

moreover, it can be easily checked that

$$
\left\langle y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1}, y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle = \frac {1}{2} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2} \| v _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.13}
$$

plugging (3.9)-(3.13) into (3.8) and rearranging the terms, we conclude that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}) - f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) \\ \leq \frac {l _ {1 2} ^ {2} \rho}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \left(\mu - \frac {1}{2 \rho} - \frac {\rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2}\right) \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \left(\frac {\mu}{2} - \frac {1}{\rho}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.14} \\ \end{array}
$$

by setting  $\beta_{k} = \eta$ ,  $b_{k} = 0$  in (2.6) of lemma 2.1 and the assumption  $\eta > l_{11}$ , we have

$$
f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \leq - \frac {\eta}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.15}
$$

the proof is completed by combining (3.14) with (3.15).

one may want to take the telescoping sum of (3.4) in order to provide a bound on  $\sum_{k}(\| x_{k + 1} - x_k\|^2 + \| y_{k + 1} - y_k\|^2)$ . however, since  $(1 / \rho + \rho l_{22}^2) / 2 \geq l_{22} \geq \mu$ , the coefficient of the third term in the r.h.s. of (3.4) is always positive. as a result, we need to further refine this relation as shown below.

lemma 3.2 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (3.1). also let us denote

$$
f _ {k + 1} := f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}), \quad s _ {k + 1} := \frac {2}{\rho^ {2} \mu} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2},
$$

$$
f _ {k + 1} := f _ {k + 1} + s _ {k + 1} - \left(\mu + \frac {7}{2 \rho} - \frac {\rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {2 l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{\mu}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2}.
$$

if  $\eta > l_{11}$ , then

$$
\begin{array}{l} f _ {k + 1} - f _ {k} \leq - \left(\frac {\eta}{2} - \frac {\rho l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\rho \mu^ {2}}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ - \left(\frac {3 \mu - \rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2} + \frac {\mu - 4 \rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2 \rho \mu}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.16} \\ \end{array}
$$

proof first by (3.6) and (3.7), we have

$$
\frac {1}{\rho} \left\langle v _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle \leq \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle , \tag {3.17}
$$

which together with (3.9) then imply that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{\rho} \langle v _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle \leq \langle \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), v _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\rangle . \tag {3.18} \\ \end{array}
$$

similar to (3.10), we can easily see that

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle \leq \frac {l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2 \mu} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {\mu}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.19}
$$

by plugging (3.11), (3.12), (3.19) into (3.18), and using the identity  $\frac{1}{\rho}\langle v_{k+1}, y_{k+1} - y_k \rangle = \frac{1}{2\rho}\|y_{k+1} - y_k\|^2 + \frac{1}{2\rho}\|v_{k+1}\|^2 - \frac{1}{2\rho}\|y_k - y_{k-1}\|^2$ , we conclude that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{2 \rho} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \rho} \| v _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \rho} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2 \mu} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {\mu}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {\rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {1}{2 \rho} \| v _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \mu \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.20} \\ \end{array}
$$

rearranging the terms of (3.20), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{2 \rho} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \rho} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2 \mu} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {\mu}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \left(\mu - \frac {\rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2}\right) \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.21} \\ \end{array}
$$

multiplying  $\frac{4}{\rho\mu}$  on both sides of (3.21) and using the definition of  $s_{k + 1}$ , we obtain

$$
s _ {k + 1} - s _ {k} \leq \frac {2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\mu^ {2} \rho} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {2}{\rho} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \left(\frac {4}{\rho} - \frac {2 l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{\mu}\right) \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}.
$$

it then follows from (3.4) in lemma 3.1 and the definition of  $f_{k}$  that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f _ {k + 1} - f _ {k} \leq - \left(\frac {\eta}{2} - \frac {\rho l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\rho \mu^ {2}}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ - \left(\frac {3 \mu - \rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2} + \frac {\mu - 4 \rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2 \rho \mu}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

we are now ready to establish the iteration complexity for the agp algorithm in the nonconvex-strongly concave setting. in particular, letting  $\nabla g(x_k,y_k)$  be defined as in definition 2.1 and  $\varepsilon >0$  be a given target accuracy, we provide a bound on  $t(\varepsilon)$ , the first iteration index to achieve an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point, i.e.,  $\| \nabla g(x_k,y_k)\| \leq \varepsilon$ , which is equivalent to

$$
t (\varepsilon) := \min  \left\{k \mid \| \nabla g \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \| \leq \varepsilon \right\}. \tag {3.22}
$$

theorem 3.1 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (3.1). if the relations  $\eta > l_{11}, \eta > l_{12}^2\rho + \frac{4l_{12}^2}{\rho\mu^2}$ , and  $\rho \leq \frac{\mu}{4l_{22}^2}$  are satisfied, then it holds that

$$
t \left(\varepsilon\right) \leq \frac {f _ {1} - f}{d _ {1} \varepsilon^ {2}},
$$

where  $d_1 \coloneqq \min \left\{\frac{\eta}{2} - \frac{\rho l_{12}^2}{2} - \frac{2l_{12}^2}{\rho\mu^2}, \frac{3\mu - \rho l_{22}^2}{2} + \frac{\mu - 4\rho l_{22}^2}{2\rho\mu}\right\} / \max \left\{\eta^2 + 2l_{12}^2, \frac{2}{\rho^2}\right\}$  and  $\underline{f} \coloneqq \underline{f} - (\mu + \frac{7}{2\rho} - \frac{\rho l_{22}^2}{2} - \frac{2l_{22}^2}{\mu})\sigma_y^2$  with  $\underline{f} \coloneqq \min_{(x,y) \in \mathcal{x} \times \mathcal{y}} f(x,y)$  and  $\sigma_y \coloneqq \max \{\|y_1 - y_2\| | y_1, y_2 \in \mathcal{y}\}$ .

proof it follows immediately from (3.1) and (3.2) that

$$
\| (\nabla g _ {k}) _ {x} \| = \eta \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \|. \tag {3.23}
$$

on the other hand, by (3.3) and the triangle inequality, we obtain that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| (\nabla g _ {k}) _ {y} \| \\ \leq \frac {1}{\rho} \| \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {y}} \left(y _ {k} + \rho \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right)\right) - \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {y}} \left(y _ {k} + \rho \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right)\right) \| + \frac {1}{\rho} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| \\ \leq l _ {1 2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| + \frac {1}{\rho} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \|. \tag {3.24} \\ \end{array}
$$

by combining (3.23) and (3.24), and using the cauchy-schwarz inequality, we obtain

$$
\left\| \nabla g _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} \leq \left(\eta^ {2} + 2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}\right) \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} + \frac {2}{\rho^ {2}} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {3.25}
$$

observing that  $d_1 > 0$ . multiplying both sides of (3.25) by  $d_1$ , and using (3.16) in lemma 3.2, we have

$$
d _ {1} \| \nabla g _ {k} \| ^ {2} \leq f _ {k} - f _ {k + 1}. \tag {3.26}
$$

summing up the above inequalities from  $k = 1$  to  $k = t(\varepsilon)$ , we obtain

$$
\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t (\varepsilon)} d _ {1} \| \nabla g _ {k} \| ^ {2} \leq f _ {1} - f _ {t (\varepsilon) + 1}. \tag {3.27}
$$

note that by the definition of  $f_{k + 1}$  in lemma 3.2, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} f _ {t (\varepsilon) + 1} = f _ {t (\varepsilon) + 1} + s _ {t (\varepsilon) + 1} - (\mu + \frac {7}{2 \rho} - \frac {\rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {2 l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{\mu}) \| y _ {t (\varepsilon) + 1} - y _ {t (\varepsilon)} \| ^ {2} \\ \geq \underline {{f}} - (\mu + \frac {7}{2 \rho} - \frac {\rho l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {2 l _ {2 2} ^ {2}}{\mu}) \sigma_ {y} ^ {2} = \underline {{f}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the inequality follows from the definitions of  $\underline{f}$  and  $\sigma_y$ , and the facts that  $s_k \geq 0$  ( $\forall k \geq 1$ ) and  $\mu + \frac{7}{2\rho} - \frac{\rho l_{22}^2}{2} - \frac{2l_{22}^2}{\mu} \geq 0$  due to the selection of  $\rho$ . we then conclude from (3.27) that  $\sum_{k=1}^{t(\varepsilon)} d_1 \| \nabla g_k \|^2 \leq f_1 - f_{t(\varepsilon)+1} \leq f_1 - \underline{f}$  which, in view of the definition of  $t(\varepsilon)$ , implies that  $\varepsilon^2 \leq (f_1 - \underline{f}) / (t(\varepsilon) \cdot d_1)$  or equivalently,  $t(\varepsilon) \leq (f_1 - \underline{f}) / (d_1 \varepsilon^2)$ .

a few remarks are in place for the results obtained in theorem 3.1. first, in view of theorem 3.1, the gradient complexity of algorithm 1 to obtain a stationary point that satisfies  $\| \nabla g(x_k,y_k)\| \leq \varepsilon$  is given by  $\mathcal{o}(l^2\varepsilon^{-2})$  under the nonconvex-strongly concave setting. second, under this setting, very few single-loop algorithms have been investigated although a few other existing algorithms can achieve similar complexity bound. in particular, it seems that even the complexity bound for the gda algorithm remains unknown under this setting when  $\mathcal{x}$  and  $\mathcal{y}$  are both convex compact sets. compared to the state-of-the-art algorithm in [32], we improve the complexity for the nonconvex-strongly concave setting by a logarithmic factor, since we do not need to solve the inner problem at each iteration. third, as mentioned earlier, algorithm 1 is a single-loop alternating gradient projection method with constant step sizes, which is very easy to implement in practice.

# 3.2 complexity analysis for general nonconvex-concave setting

in this subsection, we analyze the iteration complexity of algorithm 1 for solving (p) under the general nonconvex-concave setting. under this setting,  $\forall k\geq 1$  , we set

$$
b _ {k} = 0, \quad \beta_ {k} = \bar {\eta} + \bar {\beta} _ {k}, \quad \gamma_ {k} = \frac {1}{\bar {\rho}}, \tag {3.28}
$$

where  $\bar{\eta} > 0$ ,  $\bar{\rho} > 0$  are two constants, and  $\bar{\beta}_k$  are stepsize parameters to be defined later. we need to make the following assumption on the parameters  $c_k$ .

assumption 3.1  $\{c_k\}$  is a nonnegative monotonically decreasing sequence.

by assumption 2.1 and  $\nabla_y f_{k - 1}(x,y) = \nabla_y f(x,y) - c_{k - 1}y$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) \right\| \\ = \| \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - c _ {k - 1} (y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1}) \| \\ \leq \left(l _ {2 2} + c _ {k - 1}\right) \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \|. \tag {3.29} \\ \end{array}
$$

denoting  $l_{22}^{\prime} = l_{22} + c_{1}$ , by assumption 3.1 and (3.29), we have

$$
\left\| \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) \right\| \leq l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime} \left\| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\|.
$$

it then follows from the above inequality and the strong concavity of  $f_{k - 1}(x_k,y)$  w.r.t.  $y$  (theorem 2.1.12 in [40]) that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\rangle \\ \leq - \frac {1}{l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime} + c _ {k - 1}} \| \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) \| ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {k - 1} l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime}}{l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime} + c _ {k - 1}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.30} \\ \end{array}
$$

this is a key inequality that we will use to establish some important recursions for the agp method under the nonconvex-concave setting in the following two results. this is also one of the key differences between the proof for the nonconvex-strongly concave setting and the one for the nonconvex-concave setting.

lemma 3.3 suppose that assumptions 2.1 and 3.1 hold. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (3.28). if  $\forall k, \bar{\beta}_k > l_{11}$  and  $\bar{\rho} \leq \frac{2}{l_{22}' + c_1}$ , then

$$
\begin{array}{l} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \\ \leq - \left(\bar {\eta} + \frac {\bar {\beta} _ {k}}{2} - \frac {\bar {\rho} l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {c _ {k - 1}}{2} \left(\left\| y _ {k + 1} \right\| ^ {2} - \left\| y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}\right). \tag {3.31} \\ \end{array}
$$

proof the optimality condition for  $y_{k}$  in (2.5) implies that,  $\forall y\in \mathcal{v},\forall k\geq 1$

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k} \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \left(y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k}\right), y - y _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \leq 0. \tag {3.32}
$$

by choosing  $y = y_{k}$  in (3.32), we have

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k} \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \left(y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k}\right), y _ {k} - y _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \leq 0. \tag {3.33}
$$

on the other hand, by replacing  $k$  with  $k - 1$  and choosing  $y = y_{k + 1}$  in (3.32), we obtain

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) - \frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \left(y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1}\right), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle \leq 0. \tag {3.34}
$$

the concavity of  $f_{k}(x_{k + 1},y)$  w.r.t.  $y$  together with (3.34) then imply that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f _ {k} \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - f _ {k} \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) \\ \leq \langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k} (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle + \frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \langle y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1}, y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle \\ = \langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k} (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle + \langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), v _ {k + 1} \rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\rangle + \frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \left\langle y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1}, y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle , \tag {3.35} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $v_{k + 1} = y_{k + 1} - y_k - (y_k - y_{k - 1})$ . we now provide bounds on the inner product terms of (3.35). firstly, by the definition of  $f_{k}(x_{k + 1},y_{k})$  and  $f_{k - 1}(x_k,y_k)$ , assumptions 2.1 and 3.1, and the cauchy-schwarz inequality, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k} (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle \\ = \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle - \left(c _ {k} - c _ {k - 1}\right) \left\langle y _ {k}, y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle \\ \leq \frac {\bar {\rho} l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {k} - c _ {k - 1}}{2} \left(\| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \| y _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right) \\ + \frac {c _ {k} - c _ {k - 1}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {\bar {\rho} l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {k} - c _ {k - 1}}{2} \left(\| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \| y _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right). \tag {3.36} \\ \end{array}
$$

secondly, by the cauchy-schwarz inequality,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), v _ {k + 1} \rangle \leq \frac {\bar {\rho}}{2} \| \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| v _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.37} \\ \end{array}
$$

thirdly, it follows from (3.30) that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\rangle \\ \leq - \frac {1}{l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime} + c _ {k - 1}} \left\| \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) \right\| ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {k - 1} l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime}}{l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime} + c _ {k - 1}} \left\| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\| ^ {2} \\ \leq - \frac {1}{l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime} + c _ {k - 1}} \| \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.38} \\ \end{array}
$$

also observe that

$$
\frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \left\langle y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k}, y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\rangle = \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| v _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.39}
$$

plugging (3.36)-(3.39) into (3.35), and using the definition of  $f_{k}(x_{k + 1},y_{k + 1})$  and  $f_{k}(x_{k + 1},y_{k})$  and the assumption  $\frac{\bar{\rho}}{2} \leq \frac{1}{l_{22}' + c_1}$ , we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}) - f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) \leq \frac {\bar {\rho} l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} + \frac {c _ {k - 1}}{2} \left(\| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \| y _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right). \tag {3.40} \\ \end{array}
$$

by setting  $\beta_{k} = \bar{\eta} +\bar{\beta}_{k}$ $b_{k} = 0$  in (2.6) of lemma 2.1 and the assumption  $\bar{\beta}_k > l_{11}$ , we have

$$
f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \leq - \left(\bar {\eta} + \frac {\bar {\beta} _ {k}}{2}\right) \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {3.41}
$$

the result in (3.31) then follows by adding (3.40) and (3.41).

it turns out that from lemma 3.3 we can not obtain an upper bound on the positively weighted sum of  $\| x_{k + 1} - x_k\| ^2$  and  $\| y_{k + 1} - y_k\| ^2$  to provide an upper bound for  $\| \nabla g_k\|$ . we need to further refine this result in (3.31) to overcome this difficulty. in particular, we obtain below a new inequality as in (3.46) to further investigate the relation between  $\| x_{k + 1} - x_k\| ^2$  and  $\| y_{k + 1} - y_k\| ^2$ . then by using this new inequality, we construct

a new potential function as shown in the following important result for algorithm 1. note that in lemma 3.2 we have constructed a potential function which involves the function value plus some distance between two adjacent iterates for the nonconvex-strongly concave cases, whereas in the following lemma an additional quadratic regularization term, i.e.,  $\| y_{k + 1}\|^2$ , is added to construct another potential function for the general nonconvex-concave case.

lemma 3.4 suppose that assumptions 2.1 and 3.1 hold. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (3.28). also let us denote

$$
\mathcal {s} _ {k + 1} := \frac {8}{\bar {\rho} ^ {2} c _ {k + 1}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \left(1 - \frac {c _ {k}}{c _ {k + 1}}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2},
$$

$$
\mathcal {f} _ {k + 1} := f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}) + \mathcal {s} _ {k + 1} - \frac {1 5}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {k}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}.
$$

if

$$
\bar {\beta} _ {k} > l _ {1 1}, \frac {1}{c _ {k + 1}} - \frac {1}{c _ {k}} \leq \frac {\bar {\rho}}{5}, \bar {\rho} \leq \frac {2}{l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime} + c _ {1}}, \tag {3.42}
$$

then  $\forall k\geq 2$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathcal {f} _ {k + 1} - \mathcal {f} _ {k} \leq - \left(\bar {\eta} + \frac {\bar {\beta} _ {k}}{2} - \frac {\bar {\rho} l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {1 6 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho} c _ {k} ^ {2}}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {c _ {k - 1} - c _ {k}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {9}{1 0 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \left(\frac {c _ {k - 1}}{c _ {k}} - \frac {c _ {k}}{c _ {k + 1}}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.43} \\ \end{array}
$$

proof by (3.33) and (3.34), we have

$$
\frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \left\langle v _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle \leq \left\langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k} \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\rangle . \tag {3.44}
$$

similar to (3.35) in lemma 3.3, (3.44) can be rewritten as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \langle v _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle \\ \leq \langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k} (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}), y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \rangle + \langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), v _ {k + 1} \rangle \\ + \langle \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \rangle . \\ \end{array}
$$

using an argument similar to the proof of (3.36)-(3.39), the relation in (3.30), and the cauchy-schwarz inequality, we conclude from the above inequality that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| v _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2 a _ {k}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {a _ {k}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {k} - c _ {k - 1}}{2} \left(\| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \| y _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right) \\ + \frac {\bar {\rho}}{2} \| \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime} + c _ {k - 1}} \| \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {y} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| v _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {k - 1} l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime}}{l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime} + c _ {k - 1}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} + \frac {c _ {k} - c _ {k - 1}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2}, \tag {3.45} \\ \end{array}
$$

for any  $a_{k} > 0$ . observing by  $c_{1}\leq l_{22}^{\prime}$ , and assumption 3.1, we have

$$
- \frac {c _ {k - 1} l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime}}{c _ {k - 1} + l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime}} \leq - \frac {c _ {k - 1} l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime}}{2 l _ {2 2} ^ {\prime}} = - \frac {c _ {k - 1}}{2} \leq - \frac {c _ {k}}{2}.
$$

combining  $\bar{\rho} \leq \frac{2}{l_{22}' + c_1}$  and rearranging the terms in (3.45), we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {c _ {k} - c _ {k - 1}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {1}{2 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} + \frac {c _ {k} - c _ {k - 1}}{2} \| y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2 a _ {k}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {a _ {k}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {c _ {k}}{2} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

by multiplying  $\frac{16}{\bar{\rho}c_k}$  on both sides of the above inequality, we then obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {8}{\bar {\rho} ^ {2} c _ {k}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \left(1 - \frac {c _ {k - 1}}{c _ {k}}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {8}{\bar {\rho} ^ {2} c _ {k}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} + \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \left(1 - \frac {c _ {k - 1}}{c _ {k}}\right) \| y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {8 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho} c _ {k} a _ {k}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {8 a _ {k}}{\bar {\rho} c _ {k}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.46} \\ \end{array}
$$

setting  $a_k = \frac{c_k}{2}$  in the above inequality, and using the definition of  $s_{k+1}$  and (3.42), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathcal {s} _ {k + 1} - \mathcal {s} _ {k} \leq \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \left(\frac {c _ {k - 1}}{c _ {k}} - \frac {c _ {k}}{c _ {k + 1}}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1 6 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho} c _ {k} ^ {2}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {2 8}{5 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.47} \\ \end{array}
$$

combining (3.47) and (3.31) in lemma 3.3, and using the definition of  $\mathcal{f}_{k + 1}$ , we conclude

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathcal {f} _ {k + 1} - \mathcal {f} _ {k} \leq - \left(\bar {\eta} + \frac {\bar {\beta} _ {k}}{2} - \frac {\bar {\rho} l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {1 6 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho} c _ {k} ^ {2}}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {c _ {k - 1} - c _ {k}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {9}{1 0 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \left(\frac {c _ {k - 1}}{c _ {k}} - \frac {c _ {k}}{c _ {k + 1}}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

we are now ready to establish the iteration complexity for the agp algorithm to achieve an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point in the general nonconvex-concave setting.

theorem 3.2 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (3.28). if  $\bar{\rho} \leq \frac{1}{10l_{22}}$ ,  $\tau > \max \left\{\frac{19^2(l_{11} + 2\bar{\eta} - \bar{\rho}l_{12}^2)}{20^2 \cdot 16\bar{\rho}l_{12}^2}, 2\right\}$ ,  $c_k = \frac{19}{20\bar{\rho}k^{1/4}}$ ,  $k \geq 1$ , then for any given  $\varepsilon > 0$ ,

$$
t (\varepsilon) \leq \max \left(\left(\frac {2 \cdot 8 0 ^ {2} \bar {\rho} (\tau - 2) l _ {1 2} ^ {2} d _ {2} d _ {3}}{1 9 ^ {2} \varepsilon^ {2}} + 1\right) ^ {2}, \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {4}}{1 0 ^ {4} \bar {\rho} ^ {4} \varepsilon^ {4}}\right),
$$

where  $d_2 = \mathcal{f}_2 - \underline{\mathcal{f}} +\left(8\cdot 2^{1 / 4} + \frac{19}{40}\right)\frac{\hat{\sigma}_y^2}{\bar{\rho}}$ $\bar{d}_{1} = \frac{8\tau^{2}}{(\tau - 2)^{2}} +\frac{19^{4}\left(2\left(\bar{\rho}l_{12}^{2} - \bar{\eta}\right)^{2} + 2l_{12}^{2}\right)}{64\cdot 20^{4}\bar{\rho}^{2}(\tau - 2)^{2}l_{12}^{4}}$ $d_{3} = \max \{\bar{d}_{1},\frac{19^{2}}{1440\sqrt{2}(\tau - 2)\bar{\rho}^{2}l_{12}^{2}}\}$

$$
\hat {\sigma} _ {y} := \max  \{\| y \| \mid y \in \mathcal {y} \}, \underline {{\mathcal {f}}} := \underline {{f}} - \left(2 ^ {1 3 / 4} + 1 5 + \frac {1 9}{4 0}\right) \frac {\hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho}} \text {w i t h} \underline {{f}} := \min  _ {(x, y) \in \mathcal {x} \times \mathcal {y}} f (x, y).
$$

proof by  $\bar{\rho} \leq \frac{1}{10l_{22}}$  and  $c_k = \frac{19}{20\bar{\rho}k^{1/4}}, \forall k \geq 1$ , let us denote  $\bar{\beta}_k = \bar{\rho}l_{12}^2 + \frac{16\tau l_{12}^2}{\bar{\rho}c_k^2} - 2\bar{\eta}$ ,  $\alpha_k = \frac{8(\tau - 2)l_{12}^2}{\bar{\rho}c_k^2}$ , we can easily see that the relations in (3.42) are satisfied. it follows from the selection of  $\bar{\beta}_k$  and  $\alpha_k$  that

$$
\bar {\eta} + \frac {\bar {\beta} _ {k}}{2} - \frac {\bar {\rho} l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {1 6 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho} c _ {k} ^ {2}} = \alpha_ {k}.
$$

this observation, in view of lemma 3.4, then immediately implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \alpha_ {k} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {9}{1 0 \bar {\rho}} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} \leq \mathcal {f} _ {k} - \mathcal {f} _ {k + 1} + \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \left(\frac {c _ {k - 1}}{c _ {k}} - \frac {c _ {k}}{c _ {k + 1}}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {c _ {k - 1} - c _ {k}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {3.48} \\ \end{array}
$$

we can easily check from the definition of  $f_{k}(x,y)$  that

$$
\| \nabla g _ {k} \| - \| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \| \leq c _ {k} \| y _ {k} \|.
$$

by replacing  $f$  with  $f_{k}$ ,  $\eta$  with  $\bar{\beta}_k + \bar{\eta}$ , similar to (3.23) and (3.24), we immediately obtain that

$$
\left\| \left(\nabla \tilde {g} _ {k}\right) _ {x} \right\| = \left(\bar {\beta} _ {k} + \tilde {\eta}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \|, \tag {3.49}
$$

and

$$
\left\| \left(\nabla \tilde {g} _ {k}\right) _ {y} \right\| \leq \frac {1}{\bar {\rho}} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| + l _ {1 2} \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\|. \tag {3.50}
$$

combining (3.49) and (3.50), and using the cauchy-schwarz inequality, we have

$$
\left\| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} \leq \left(\left(\bar {\beta} _ {k} + \bar {\eta}\right) ^ {2} + 2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}\right) \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} + \frac {2}{\bar {\rho} ^ {2}} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {3.51}
$$

since both  $\alpha_{k}$  and  $\bar{\beta}_k$  are in the same order when  $k$  becomes large enough, it then follows from the definition of  $\bar{d}_1$  that  $\forall k\geq 1$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\left(\bar {\beta} _ {k} + \bar {\eta}\right) ^ {2} + 2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\alpha_ {k} ^ {2}} = \frac {\left(\bar {\rho} l _ {1 2} ^ {2} + \frac {1 6 \tau l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho} c _ {k} ^ {2}} - \bar {\eta}\right) ^ {2} + 2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\alpha_ {k} ^ {2}} \\ \leq \frac {2 \left(\frac {1 6 \tau l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho} c _ {k} ^ {2}}\right) ^ {2} + 2 (\bar {\rho} l _ {1 2} ^ {2} - \bar {\eta}) ^ {2} + 2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\alpha_ {k} ^ {2}} \\ = \frac {8 \tau^ {2}}{(\tau - 2) ^ {2}} + \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \left(2 \left(\bar {\rho} l _ {1 2} ^ {2} - \bar {\eta}\right) ^ {2} + 2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}\right)}{6 4 \cdot 2 0 ^ {4} \bar {\rho} ^ {2} (\tau - 2) ^ {2} l _ {1 2} ^ {4} k} \leq \bar {d} _ {1}. \tag {3.52} \\ \end{array}
$$

combining the previous two inequalities in (3.52) and (3.51), we obtain

$$
\left\| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} \leq \bar {d} _ {1} \left(\alpha_ {k}\right) ^ {2} \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} + \frac {2}{\bar {\rho} ^ {2}} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {3.53}
$$

denote  $d_k^{(2)} = \frac{1}{\max\left\{\overline{d}_1\alpha_k,\frac{20}{9\rho}\right\}}$ . by multiplying  $d_k^{(2)}$  on the both sides of (3.53), and using (3.48), we have

$$
d _ {k} ^ {(2)} \| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \leq \mathcal {f} _ {k} - \mathcal {f} _ {k + 1} + \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \left(\frac {c _ {k - 1}}{c _ {k}} - \frac {c _ {k}}{c _ {k + 1}}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} + \frac {c _ {k - 1} - c _ {k}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}, \tag {3.54}
$$

where the last inequality follows since  $d_k^{(2)}$  is a decreasing sequence. denoting

$$
\tilde {t} (\varepsilon) := \min  \left\{k \mid \| \nabla \tilde {g} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \| \leq \frac {\varepsilon}{2}, k \geq 2 \right\},
$$

summing both sides of (3.54) from  $k = 2$  to  $k = \tilde{t}(\varepsilon)$ , we then obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\tilde {t} (\varepsilon)} d _ {k} ^ {(2)} \left\| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} \\ \leq \mathcal {f} _ {2} - \mathcal {f} _ {\tilde {t} (\varepsilon)} + \frac {8}{\bar {\rho}} \left(\frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} - \frac {c _ {\tilde {t} (\varepsilon)}}{c _ {\tilde {t} (\varepsilon) + 1}}\right) \hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2} + \frac {c _ {1} - c _ {\tilde {t} (\varepsilon)}}{2} \hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2} \\ \leq \mathcal {f} _ {2} - \mathcal {f} _ {\tilde {t} (\varepsilon)} + \frac {8 c _ {1}}{\bar {\rho} c _ {2}} \hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2} + \frac {c _ {1}}{2} \hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2} \\ = \mathcal {f} _ {2} - \mathcal {f} _ {\tilde {t} (\varepsilon)} + \left(8 \cdot 2 ^ {1 / 4} + \frac {1 9}{4 0}\right) \frac {\hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho}}. \tag {3.55} \\ \end{array}
$$

note that by the definition of  $\mathcal{f}_{k + 1}$  in lemma 3.4, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathcal {f} _ {\tilde {t} (\varepsilon)} \geq \underline {{f}} - \frac {8 \hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho}} \left(1 + \frac {1}{\tilde {t} (\varepsilon)}\right) ^ {1 / 4} - \frac {1 5 \hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho}} - \frac {c _ {1} \hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2}}{2} \\ = \underline {{f}} - \left(2 ^ {1 3 / 4} + 1 5 + \frac {1 9}{4 0}\right) \frac {\hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho}} = \underline {{\mathcal {f}}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\underline{f} \coloneqq \min_{(x,y) \in \mathcal{x} \times \mathcal{y}} f(x,y)$ . we then conclude from (3.55) that

$$
\sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\tilde {t} (\varepsilon)} d _ {k} ^ {(2)} \| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \leq \mathcal {f} _ {2} - \underline {{\mathcal {f}}} + \left(8 \cdot 2 ^ {1 / 4} + \frac {1 9}{4 0}\right) \frac {\hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {2}}{\bar {\rho}} = d _ {2}. \tag {3.56}
$$

we can see from the selection of  $d_{3}$  that  $d_{3} = \max \{\bar{d}_{1},\frac{20}{9\rho\alpha_{2}}\}$ . observe that  $\alpha_{k}$  is an increasing sequence, when  $k\geq 2$ , we have that  $d_{3}\geq \max \{\bar{d}_{1},\frac{20}{9\rho\alpha_{k}}\}$ , which implies that  $d_k^{(2)}\geq \frac{1}{d_3\alpha_k}$ , by multiplying  $d_{3}$  on the both sides of (3.56), and combining the definition of  $d_{2}$ , we have  $\sum_{k = 2}^{\tilde{t} (\varepsilon)}\frac{1}{\alpha_k}\| \nabla \tilde{g}_k\|^2\leq d_2d_3$ , which, by the definition of  $\tilde{t} (\varepsilon)$ , implies that

$$
\frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{4} \leq \frac {d _ {2} d _ {3}}{\sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\tilde {t} (\varepsilon)} \frac {1}{\alpha_ {k}}}. \tag {3.57}
$$

note that when  $c_{k} = \frac{19}{20\bar{\rho}k^{1 / 4}}$ ,  $\alpha_{k} = \frac{2\cdot 40^{2}\bar{\rho}(\tau - 2)l_{12}^{2}\sqrt{k}}{19^{2}}$ . by using the fact  $\sum_{k = 2}^{\tilde{t} (\varepsilon)}1 / \sqrt{k}\geq \sqrt{\tilde{t}(\varepsilon)} -1$  and (3.57), we conclude  $\frac{\varepsilon^2}{4}\leq \frac{2\cdot 40^2\bar{\rho}(\tau - 2)l_{12}^2d_2d_3}{19^2\left(\sqrt{\tilde{t}(\varepsilon)} - 1\right)}$  or equivalently,

$$
\tilde {t} (\varepsilon) \leq \left(\frac {2 \cdot 8 0 ^ {2} \bar {\rho} (\tau - 2) l _ {1 2} ^ {2} d _ {2} d _ {3}}{1 9 ^ {2} \varepsilon^ {2}} + 1\right) ^ {2}.
$$

on the other hand, if  $k \geq \frac{19^4 \hat{\sigma}_y^4}{10^4 \bar{\rho}^4 \varepsilon^4}$ , then  $c_k = \frac{19}{20 \bar{\rho} k^{1/4}} \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2 \hat{\sigma}_y}$ . this inequality together with the definition of  $\hat{\sigma}_y$  then imply that  $c_k \| y_k \| \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ . therefore, there exists a

$$
\begin{array}{l} t (\varepsilon) \leq \max  (\tilde {t} (\varepsilon), \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {4}}{1 0 ^ {4} \bar {\rho} ^ {4} \varepsilon^ {4}}) \\ \leq \max \left(\left(\frac {2 \cdot 8 0 ^ {2} \bar {\rho} (\tau - 2) l _ {1 2} ^ {2} d _ {2} d _ {3}}{1 9 ^ {2} \varepsilon^ {2}} + 1\right) ^ {2}, \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \hat {\sigma} _ {y} ^ {4}}{1 0 ^ {4} \bar {\rho} ^ {4} \varepsilon^ {4}}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

such that  $\| \nabla g_k\| \leq \| \nabla \tilde{g}_k\| +c_k\| y_k\| \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2} +\frac{\varepsilon}{2} = \varepsilon$

according to theorem 3.2, we can show that, by specifying  $\gamma_{k}$  and  $c_{k}$  as in the order of  $k^{1 / 2}$  and  $1 / k^{1 / 4}$ , the gradient complexity of algorithm 1 to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationarity point of  $f$  in the nonconvex-concave setting can be bounded by  $\mathcal{o}(l^4\varepsilon^{-4})$ . in this setting the stepsize for updating  $x_{k}$  is in the order of  $k^{-1 / 2}$ , while the one for updating  $y_{k}$  is a constant at iteration  $k$ .

it is worth mentioning two closely related works to ours. one is the hibsa algorithm proposed in [33] for nonconvex-concave problems. it seems that our iteration complexity is slightly better than that of the hibsa algorithm by a logarithmic factor. more importantly, the two subproblems for updating  $x_{k}$  and  $y_{k}$  in agp are much easier than those in the hibsa algorithm, as the latter method needs to solve  $\max_{y\in \mathcal{v}}f(x,y)$  at each iteration to update  $y_{k}$  under the general nonconvex-concave (not necessarily strongly concave) setting. the other related method is the gda algorithm. by setting the stepsize to update  $x_{k}$  in the order of  $\varepsilon^4$ , lin et al. [31] proved that the iteration complexity of gda to return an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of  $\varphi(\cdot)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{v}}f(\cdot,y)$  is bounded by  $\tilde{\mathcal{o}}(\varepsilon^{-6})$  for nonconvex-concave minimax problems when  $\mathcal{x}=\mathbb{r}^{n}$  and  $\mathcal{y}$  is a convex compact set. in this paper, both  $\mathcal{x}$  and  $\mathcal{y}$  are convex compact sets, hence the outer problem is a constrained minimization problem instead of an unconstrained one. under this setting, the stopping rule, i.e.,  $\| \nabla \varphi(\cdot)\| \leq \varepsilon$ , used in [31] for unconstrained minimization setting needs to be reconsidered. moreover, the step sizes to update  $x_{k}$  in agp is a decreasing sequence in the order of  $k^{-1/2}$ , which is significantly larger than  $\mathcal{o}(\varepsilon^4)$  stepsize allowed in gda, and thus may result in better practical performance.

note that for solving unconstrained bilinear minimax problem, the alternating gda algorithm with any fixed step size will cause recurrence [6]. in the classic convex optimization literature, such a divergence issue was usually handled by incorporating averaging, smoothing, or direct acceleration techniques (see, e.g., sections 3.5-3.8 and sections 4.3-4.5 of [26]). however, the proposed agp algorithm for nonconvex minimax problems is not equivalent to the alternating gda algorithm, since a regularized version of the original function is incorporated, and a variable stepsize policy has been used for the nonconvex-concave setting. hence, the results of our paper do not contradict with existing ones.

# 4 complexity analysis for convex-nonconcave minimax problems

in this section, we establish the convergence of agp algorithm for the cases where  $f$  is convex w.r.t.  $x$ , but possibly nonconcave w.r.t.  $y$ . these are important minimax problems but the studies on their solution methods are still quite limited. although there exists some symmetry between nonconvex-concave and convex-nonconcave minimax problems, the complexity analysis of the same agp algorithm for nonconvex-concave setting cannot be trivially extended to that for convex-nonconcave setting.

# 4.1 strongly convex-nonconcave setting

in this subsection, we analyze the iteration complexity of algorithm 1 for solving strongly convex-nonconcave minimax optimization problems (p), i.e.,  $f(x,y)$  is  $\theta$ -strongly convex w.r.t.  $x$  for any fixed  $y \in \mathcal{v}$ , and nonconcave w.r.t.  $y$  for any given  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ . under this setting,  $\forall k \geq 1$ , we set

$$
\beta_ {k} = \frac {1}{\zeta}, \gamma_ {k} = \nu , b _ {k} = c _ {k} = 0, \tag {4.1}
$$

in algorithm 1, and simplify the update for  $x_{k}$  and  $y_{k}$  as follows:

$$
x _ {k + 1} = \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {x}} \left(x _ {k} - \zeta \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right)\right), \tag {4.2}
$$

$$
y _ {k + 1} = \mathcal {p} _ {\mathcal {y}} \left(y _ {k} + \frac {1}{\nu} \nabla_ {y} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right)\right). \tag {4.3}
$$

our goal in the remaining part of this subsection is to establish the iteration complexity of algorithm 1 under the strongly convex-nonconcave setting. the convergence analysis for this setting is different from that of the nonconvex-strongly concave setting in section 3.

lemma 4.1 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (4.1). if  $\nu > l_{22}$ , then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \\ \geq \frac {\nu}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \zeta}{2} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} + (\theta - \frac {1}{2 \zeta} - \frac {\zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2}) \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ + \left(\frac {\theta}{2} - \frac {1}{\zeta}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.4} \\ \end{array}
$$

proof similar to (3.5)-(3.7) in the proof of lemma 3.1, by the optimality condition for  $x_{k}$  in (4.2) implies that  $\forall x \in \mathcal{x}$  and  $\forall k \geq 1$ ,

$$
\langle \nabla_ {x} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) + \frac {1}{\zeta} (x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k}), x - x _ {k + 1} \rangle \geq 0, \tag {4.5}
$$

$$
\langle \nabla_ {x} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) + \frac {1}{\zeta} (x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k}), x _ {k} - x _ {k + 1} \rangle \geq 0, \tag {4.6}
$$

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}\right) + \frac {1}{\zeta} \left(x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1}\right), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\rangle \geq 0, \tag {4.7}
$$

which, in view of the fact that  $f(x,y)$  is  $\theta$ -strongly convex w.r.t.  $x$  for any given  $y \in \mathcal{v}$ , then implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) \\ \geq \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\rangle + \frac {\theta}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ \geq \langle \nabla_ {x} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {x} f (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \rangle \\ - \frac {1}{\zeta} \left\langle x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1}, x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\rangle + \frac {\theta}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.8} \\ \end{array}
$$

denoting  $m_{k + 1}\coloneqq (x_{k + 1} - x_k) - (x_k - x_{k - 1})$ , by assumption 2.1, the cauchy-schwarz inequality and the  $\theta$ -strongly convexity of  $f$  w.r.t.  $x$ , we can estimate the first inner product term in the r.h.s. of (4.8) as

$$
