\begin{array}{l} \langle \nabla_ {x} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {x} f (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \rangle \\ = \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) - \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}\right), m _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) - \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}\right), x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \right\rangle \tag {4.9} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \geq - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \zeta}{2} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \zeta} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {\zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {1}{2 \zeta} \| m _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} + \theta \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.10} \\ \end{array}
$$

moreover, it can be easily checked that

$$
\langle x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1}, x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \rangle = \frac {1}{2} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2} \| m _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.11}
$$

plugging (4.10) and (4.11) into (4.8) and rearranging the terms, we conclude that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) \geq - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \zeta}{2} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} + (\theta - \frac {1}{2 \zeta} - \frac {\zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2}) \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ + \left(\frac {\theta}{2} - \frac {1}{\zeta}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.12} \\ \end{array}
$$

by setting  $\gamma_{k} = \nu$ ,  $c_{k} = 0$  in (2.7) of lemma 2.1 and the assumption  $\nu > l_{22}$ , we have

$$
f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) \geq \frac {\nu}{2} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {4.13}
$$

the proof is completed by combining (4.12) with (4.13).

next, we further refine this relation in (4.4) as shown below. note that in lemma 3.2 we have constructed a potential function for the nonconvex-strongly concave case, whereas in the following lemma, an additional term involving the distance between two adjacent iterates, i.e.,  $\| x_{k + 1} - x_k\|^2$ , is added to construct another potential function for the strongly convex-nonconcave case.

lemma 4.2 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (4.1). denote

$$
\hat {f} _ {k + 1} := f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right), \hat {s} _ {k + 1} := - \frac {2}{\zeta^ {2} \theta} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2},
$$

$$
\hat {f} _ {k + 1} := \hat {f} _ {k + 1} + \hat {s} _ {k + 1} + (\theta + \frac {7}{2 \zeta} - \frac {\zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {2 l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{\theta}) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - (\frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \zeta}{2} + \frac {2 l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{\theta^ {2} \zeta}) \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2}.
$$

if  $\nu > l_{22}$ , then  $\forall k \geq 1$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {f} _ {k + 1} - \hat {f} _ {k} \geq \left(\frac {\nu}{2} - \frac {\zeta l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {2 l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{\zeta \theta^ {2}}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ + \left(\frac {3 \theta - \zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2} + \frac {\theta - 4 \zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2 \zeta \theta}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.14} \\ \end{array}
$$

proof first by (4.6) and (4.7), we have

$$
\frac {1}{\zeta} \left\langle m _ {k + 1}, x _ {k} - x _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \geq \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) - \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}\right), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\rangle , \tag {4.15}
$$

which together with (4.9) then imply that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{\zeta} \langle m _ {k + 1}, x _ {k} - x _ {k + 1} \rangle \geq \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {x} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) - \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}\right), m _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) - \nabla_ {x} f \left(x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}\right), x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \right\rangle (4.16) \\ \geq - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{2 \theta} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {\theta}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {\zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {1}{2 \zeta} \| m _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} + \theta \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}, (4.17) \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second inequality is similar to the proof of (4.10) except that for the first term in the r.h.s., we use  $\langle \nabla_x f(x_k, y_k) - \nabla_x f(x_k, y_{k-1}), x_{k+1} - x_k \rangle \geq -\frac{l_{21}^2}{2\theta} \|y_k - y_{k-1}\|^2 - \frac{\theta}{2} \|x_{k+1} - x_k\|^2$ . by using the identity  $\frac{1}{\zeta} \langle m_{k+1}, x_k - x_{k+1} \rangle = \frac{1}{2\zeta} \|x_k - x_{k-1}\|^2 - \frac{1}{2\zeta} \|x_{k+1} - x_k\|^2 - \frac{1}{2\zeta} \|m_{k+1}\|^2$ , we conclude from (4.17) that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{2 \zeta} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \zeta} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \zeta} \| m _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ \geq - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{2 \theta} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {\theta}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {\zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {1}{2 \zeta} \| m _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} + \theta \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.18} \\ \end{array}
$$

rearranging the terms of (4.18), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{2 \zeta} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \zeta} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ \geq - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{2 \theta} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {\theta}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \left(\theta - \frac {\zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2}\right) \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.19} \\ \end{array}
$$

multiplying  $\frac{4}{\zeta\theta}$  on both sides of (4.19) and using the definition of  $\hat{s}_{k + 1}$ , we obtain

$$
\hat {s} _ {k + 1} - \hat {s} _ {k} \geq - \frac {2 l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{\theta^ {2} \zeta} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {2}{\zeta} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \left(\frac {4}{\zeta} - \frac {2 l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{\theta}\right) \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}.
$$

the proof is completed by (4.4) in lemma 4.1 and the definition of  $\hat{f}_k$ .

we are now ready to establish the iteration complexity for the agp algorithm in the strongly convex-nonconcave setting.

theorem 4.1 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (4.1). if the relations  $\nu > l_{22}, \nu > l_{21}^2\zeta + \frac{4l_{21}^2}{\zeta\theta^2}$ ,  $\zeta \leq \frac{\theta}{4l_{11}^2}$  are satisfied, then  $\forall \varepsilon > 0$ , it holds that

$$
t \left(\varepsilon\right) \leq \frac {\overline {{f}} - \hat {f} _ {1}}{\hat {d} _ {1} \varepsilon^ {2}},
$$

where  $\hat{d}_1 := \frac{\min\left\{\frac{\nu}{2} - \frac{\zeta l_{21}^2}{2} - \frac{2l_{21}^2}{\zeta\theta^2}, \frac{3\theta - \zeta l_{11}^2}{2} + \frac{\theta - 4\zeta l_{11}^2}{2\zeta\theta}\right\}}{\max\left\{\frac{1}{\zeta^2} + 2l_{12}^2, 2\nu^2\right\}}$  and  $\overline{f} = \bar{f} + (\theta + \frac{7}{2\zeta} - \frac{\zeta l_{11}^2}{2} - \frac{2l_{11}^2}{\theta})\sigma_x^2$  with  $\overline{f} := \max_{(x,y) \in \mathcal{x} \times \mathcal{y}} f(x,y)$ .

and  $\sigma_{x} = \max \{\| x_{1} - x_{2}\| \mid \forall x_{1},x_{2}\in \mathcal{x}\}$

proof by (4.2), we immediately obtain

$$
\left\| \left(\nabla g _ {k}\right) _ {x} \right\| \leq \frac {1}{\zeta} \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\|. \tag {4.20}
$$

on the other hand, similar to the proof of (3.24), by (4.3) and the triangle inequality, and the nonexpansiveness of the projection operator  $\mathcal{p}_{\gamma}$ , we conclude that

$$
\left\| \left(\nabla g _ {k}\right) _ {y} \right\| \leq \nu \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| + l _ {1 2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \|. \tag {4.21}
$$

by combining (4.20) and (4.21), and using the cauchy-schwarz inequality, we obtain

$$
\left\| \nabla g _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} \leq 2 \nu^ {2} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} + \left(\frac {1}{\zeta^ {2}} + 2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}\right) \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {4.22}
$$

observing that  $\hat{d}_1 > 0$ . multiplying both sides of (4.22) by  $\hat{d}_1$ , and using (4.14) in lemma 4.2, we have

$$
\hat {d} _ {1} \| \nabla g _ {k} \| ^ {2} \leq \hat {f} _ {k + 1} - \hat {f} _ {k}. \tag {4.23}
$$

summing up the above inequalities from  $k = 1$  to  $k = t(\varepsilon)$ , we obtain

$$
\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t (\varepsilon)} \hat {d} _ {1} \| \nabla g _ {k} \| ^ {2} \leq \hat {f} _ {t (\varepsilon) + 1} - \hat {f} _ {1}. \tag {4.24}
$$

note that by the definition of  $\hat{f}_{k + 1}$  in lemma 4.2, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {f} _ {t (\varepsilon) + 1} \leq \hat {f} _ {t (\varepsilon) + 1} + \left(\theta + \frac {7}{2 \zeta} - \frac {\zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {2 l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{\theta}\right) \| x _ {t (\varepsilon) + 1} - x _ {t (\varepsilon)} \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \bar {f} + (\theta + \frac {7}{2 \zeta} - \frac {\zeta l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {2 l _ {1 1} ^ {2}}{\theta}) \sigma_ {x} ^ {2} = \bar {f}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality follows from the definitions of  $\bar{f}$  and  $\sigma_{x}$ , and  $\theta +\frac{7}{2\zeta} -\frac{\zeta l_{11}^2}{2} -\frac{2l_{11}^2}{\theta}\geq 0$  due to the selection of  $\zeta$ . we then conclude from (4.24) that  $\sum_{k = 1}^{t(\varepsilon)}\hat{d}_1\| \nabla g_k\| ^2\leq \bar{f} -\hat{f}_1$  which, in view of the definition of  $t(\varepsilon)$ , implies that  $\varepsilon^2\leq (\bar{f} -\hat{f}_1) / (t(\varepsilon)\cdot \hat{d}_1)$  or equivalently,  $t(\varepsilon)\leq (\bar{f} -\hat{f}_1) / (\hat{d}_1\varepsilon^2)$ .

theorem 4.1 shows that the number of gradient evaluations performed by algorithm 1 to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of  $f$  is bounded by  $\mathcal{o}\left(l^2\varepsilon^{-2}\right)$  under the strongly convex-nonconcave setting. to the best of our knowledge, this is the first theoretical guarantee that has been obtained in the literature for solving this class of minimax problems.

# 4.2 complexity analysis for convex-nonconcave setting

in this subsection, we analyze the iteration complexity of algorithm 1 applied to the general convex-nonconcave setting, for which  $f(x,y)$  is convex w.r.t.  $x$  for any fixed  $y \in \mathcal{v}$ , and nonconcave w.r.t.  $y$  for any given  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ . under this setting,  $\forall k \geq 1$ , we set

$$
\beta_ {k} = \frac {1}{\zeta}, \gamma_ {k} = \bar {\nu} + \bar {\gamma} _ {k}, b _ {k} = q _ {k}, c _ {k} = 0, \tag {4.25}
$$

where  $\bar{\gamma}_k$  and  $q_{k}$  are stepsize parameters to be defined later. we need to make the following assumption on the parameters  $q_{k}$ .

assumption 4.1  $\{q_k\}$  is a nonnegative monotonically decreasing sequence.

by assumption 2.1 and  $\nabla_{x}f_{k - 1}(x,y) = \nabla_{x}f(x,y) + q_{k - 1}x$ , we have

$$
\left\| \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}\right) \right\| \leq \left(l _ {1 1} + q _ {k - 1}\right) \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \|. \tag {4.26}
$$

denoting  $l_{11}^{\prime} = l_{11} + q_{1}$ , by assumption 4.1 and (4.26), we have

$$
\| \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}) \| \leq l _ {1 1} ^ {'} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \}.
$$

it then follows from the above inequality and the strong convexity of  $f_{k - 1}(x,y_{k - 1})$  w.r.t.  $x$  (theorem 2.1.12 in [40]) that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}), x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \rangle \\ \geq \frac {1}{l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime} + q _ {k - 1}} \| \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}\right) \| ^ {2} + \frac {q _ {k - 1} l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime}}{l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime} + q _ {k - 1}} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.27} \\ \end{array}
$$

by using the strong convexity of  $f_{k-1}(x, y_{k-1})$  w.r.t.  $x$  instead of the strong concavity of  $f_{k-1}(x_k, y)$  w.r.t.  $y$ , this inequality provides a lower bound for the inner product instead of an upper bound shown as in (3.30). this is a key inequality that we will use to establish some important recursions for the agp algorithm under the convex-nonconcave setting in the following two results.

similar to lemma 4.1, we first provide an estimate on the increase of the function values from  $f(x_{k},y_{k})$  to  $f(x_{k + 1},y_{k + 1})$ .

lemma 4.3 suppose that assumption 2.1 and 4.1 hold. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (4.25). if  $\forall k, \bar{\gamma}_k > l_{22}$  and  $\bar{\zeta} \leq \frac{2}{l_{11}' + q_1}$ , then

$$
\begin{array}{l} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \\ \geq \left(\bar {\nu} + \frac {\bar {\gamma} _ {k}}{2}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \bar {\zeta}}{2} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{\bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {q _ {k - 1}}{2} \left(\| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \| x _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right). \tag {4.28} \\ \end{array}
$$

proof similar to (4.5)-(4.9) in the proof of lemma 4.1, by replacing  $f$  with  $f_{k}$ ,  $\zeta$  with  $\bar{\zeta}$  respectively, and setting  $\theta = 0$ , we obtain that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f _ {k} \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - f _ {k} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \\ \geq \langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \rangle + \langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}), m _ {k + 1} \rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}\right), x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \right\rangle - \frac {1}{\bar {\zeta}} \left\langle x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1}, x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\rangle . \tag {4.29} \\ \end{array}
$$

next, we prove lower bound for the four terms in the r.h.s. of (4.29) which are different from that in lemma 4.1. by using the convexity of  $f_{k-1}(x,y_{k-1})$  w.r.t  $x$  instead of the concavity of  $f_{k-1}(x_k,y)$  w.r.t  $y$ , the opposite side cauchy-schwarz inequality, and replacing  $\bar{\rho}, c_k, c_{k-1}, l_{12}, l_{22}'$  by  $\bar{\zeta}, q_k, q_{k-1}, l_{21}, l_{11}'$  respectively, assumptions 2.1 and 4.1, similar to the proof of (3.36)-(3.38) we conclude that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \rangle \\ \geq - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \bar {\zeta}}{2} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {q _ {k} - q _ {k - 1}}{2} \left(\| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \| x _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right), \tag {4.30} \\ \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}), m _ {k + 1} \rangle \geq - \frac {\bar {\zeta}}{2} \| \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}) \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| m _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}, \tag {4.31} \\ \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}), x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \rangle \\ \geq \frac {1}{l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime} + q _ {k - 1}} \| \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}) \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.32} \\ \end{array}
$$

moreover, it can be easily checked that

$$
\frac {1}{\bar {\zeta}} \left\langle x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k}, x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \right\rangle = \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| m _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.33}
$$

plugging (4.30)-(4.33) into (4.29), and by the definition of  $f_{k}(x_{k + 1},y_{k})$  and  $f_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})$  and  $\frac{\zeta}{2} \leq \frac{1}{l_{11}' + q_1}$ , we conclude that

$$
f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - f \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \geq - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \bar {\zeta}}{2} \left\| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \right\| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{\bar {\zeta}} \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \left\| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \right\| ^ {2} - \frac {q _ {k - 1}}{2} \left(\left\| x _ {k + 1} \right\| ^ {2} - \left\| x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}\right). \tag {4.34}
$$

by setting  $\gamma_{k} = \bar{\nu} +\bar{\gamma}_{k}$ $c_{k} = 0$  in (2.7) of lemma 2.1 and the assumption  $\bar{\gamma}_k > l_{22}$ , we have

$$
f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - f \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) \geq \left(\bar {\nu} + \frac {\bar {\gamma} _ {k}}{2}\right) \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {4.35}
$$

the proof is completed by combing (4.34) and (4.35).

we need to further refine the relation in (4.28) in order to establish the convergence of the agp algorithm as shown below. note that in lemma 4.2 we have constructed a potential function which involves the function value plus some distance between two adjacent iterates for the strongly convex-nonconcave case, whereas in the following lemma an additional quadratic regularization term, i.e.,  $\| x_{k + 1} \|^2$ , is added to construct another potential function for the general convex-nonconcave case.

lemma 4.4 suppose that assumptions 2.1 and 4.1 hold. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (4.25). denote

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {\mathcal {s}} _ {k + 1} := - \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta} ^ {2} q _ {k + 1}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta}} \left(1 - \frac {q _ {k}}{q _ {k + 1}}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}, \\ \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {k + 1} := f (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}) + \hat {\mathcal {s}} _ {k + 1} + \frac {1 5}{2 \bar {\xi}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {q _ {k}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \left(\frac {\bar {\zeta} l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{2} + \frac {1 6 l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{\bar {\zeta} (q _ {k + 1}) ^ {2}}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

if

$$
\bar {\gamma} _ {k} > l _ {2 2}, \frac {1}{q _ {k + 1}} - \frac {1}{q _ {k}} \leq \frac {\bar {\zeta}}{5}, \bar {\zeta} \leq \frac {2}{l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime} + q _ {1}}, \tag {4.36}
$$

then  $\forall k\geq 1$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {k + 1} - \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {k} \\ \geq \left(\bar {\nu} + \frac {\bar {\gamma} _ {k}}{2} - \frac {\bar {\zeta} l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {1 6 l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{\bar {\zeta} (q _ {k + 1}) ^ {2}}\right) \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {q _ {k} - q _ {k - 1}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {9}{1 0 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta}} \left(\frac {q _ {k}}{q _ {k + 1}} - \frac {q _ {k - 1}}{q _ {k}}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

proof similar to the proof of (4.16), by replacing  $f$  with  $f_{k}$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{\bar {\zeta}} \langle m _ {k + 1}, x _ {k} - x _ {k + 1} \rangle \geq \langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k} (x _ {k}, y _ {k}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}), x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \rangle \\ + \left\langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}\right) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} \left(x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}\right), m _ {k + 1} \right\rangle \\ + \langle \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}), x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \rangle . \\ \end{array}
$$

using an argument similar to the proof of (4.30)-(4.33), and the cauchy-schwarz inequality, we conclude from the above inequality that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| m _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ \geq - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{2 \bar {a} _ {k}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {\bar {a} _ {k}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {q _ {k} - q _ {k - 1}}{2} \left(\| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \| x _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right) \\ - \frac {q _ {k} - q _ {k - 1}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {\bar {\zeta}}{2} \| \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}) \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {1}{l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime} + q _ {k - 1}} \| \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k}, y _ {k - 1}) - \nabla_ {x} f _ {k - 1} (x _ {k - 1}, y _ {k - 1}) \| ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2 \zeta} \| m _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {q _ {k - 1} l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime}}{l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime} + q _ {k - 1}} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}, \tag {4.37} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\bar{a}_k > 0$ . observing that  $q_1 \leq l_{11}^{\prime}$  and by assumption 4.1, we have

$$
\frac {q _ {k - 1} l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime}}{q _ {k - 1} + l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime}} \geq \frac {q _ {k - 1} l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime}}{2 l _ {1 1} ^ {\prime}} = \frac {q _ {k - 1}}{2} \geq \frac {q _ {k}}{2}.
$$

by  $\bar{\zeta} \leq \frac{2}{l_{11}' + q_1}$  and assumption 4.1, rearranging the terms in (4.37), we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {q _ {k} - q _ {k - 1}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ \geq - \frac {1}{2 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {q _ {k} - q _ {k - 1}}{2} \| x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{2 \bar {a} _ {k}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {\bar {a} _ {k}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {q _ {k}}{2} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

by multiplying  $\frac{16}{\zeta q_k}$  on both sides of the above inequality, we then obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} - \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta} ^ {2} q _ {k}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta}} \left(1 - \frac {q _ {k - 1}}{q _ {k}}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ \geq - \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta} ^ {2} q _ {k}} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta}} \left(1 - \frac {q _ {k - 1}}{q _ {k}}\right) \| x _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \frac {8 l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{\bar {\zeta} q _ {k} \bar {a} _ {k}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {8 \bar {a} _ {k}}{\bar {\zeta} q _ {k}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.38} \\ \end{array}
$$

setting  $\bar{a}_k = \frac{q_k}{2}$  in the above inequality, and using the definition of  $\hat{s}_{k + 1}$  and (4.36), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {\mathcal {s}} _ {k + 1} - \hat {\mathcal {s}} _ {k} \geq \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta}} \left(\frac {q _ {k}}{q _ {k + 1}} - \frac {q _ {k - 1}}{q _ {k}}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {1 6 l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{\bar {\zeta} (q _ {k}) ^ {2}} \| y _ {k} - y _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2} \\ - \frac {2 8}{5 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k} - x _ {k - 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.39} \\ \end{array}
$$

the proof is completed by combining (4.39) and (4.28) in lemma 4.3, and using the definition of  $\hat{\mathcal{f}}_{k + 1}$ .

note that the proof of lemma 4.4 is different from that of lemma 3.4, since different potential functions, i.e.,  $\hat{\mathcal{f}}_{k + 1}$  and  $\mathcal{f}_{k + 1}$  respectively, are used to establish the convergence of the proposed algorithms. the construction of these potential functions is the key step for our convergence analysis of agp algorithms. we are now ready to establish the iteration complexity for the agp algorithm to achieve an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point for solving (p) under general convex-nonconcave setting.

theorem 4.2 suppose that assumptions 2.1 hold. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 1 with parameter settings in (4.25). if  $\bar{\zeta} \leq \frac{1}{10l_{11}}$ ,  $\tau > \max \left\{\frac{19^2(l_{22} + 2\bar{\nu} - \bar{\zeta}l_{21}^2)}{20^2 \cdot 16\bar{\zeta}l_{21}^2}, 2\right\}$ ,  $q_k = \frac{19}{20\bar{\zeta}k^{1/4}}, k \geq 1$ , then for any given  $\varepsilon > 0$ ,

$$
t (\varepsilon) \leq \max \left(\left(\frac {2 \cdot 8 0 ^ {2} \bar {\zeta} (\tau - 2) l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \hat {d} _ {3} \hat {d} _ {4}}{1 9 ^ {2} \varepsilon^ {2}} + 2\right) ^ {2}, \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {4}}{1 0 ^ {4} \bar {\zeta} ^ {4} \varepsilon^ {4}}\right),
$$

where  $\hat{\sigma}_x\coloneqq \max \{\| x\| \mid x\in \mathcal{x}\}$

$$
\hat {d} _ {3} := \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {0} - \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {2} + \frac {8 \cdot 2 ^ {1 / 4}}{\bar {\zeta}} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2} + \frac {1 9}{4 0 \bar {\zeta}} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2},
$$

$\hat{d}_4 := \max \{\hat{d}_1, \frac{10 + 20\bar{\zeta}^2l_{21}^2}{9\bar{\zeta}p_2}\}$  with  $\hat{\mathcal{d}}_1 := \frac{16\tau^2}{(\tau - 2)^2} + \frac{19^4(\bar{\zeta}l_{21}^2 - \bar{\nu})^2}{16\cdot 20^4(\tau - 2)^2l_{21}^4\bar{\zeta}^2}$ ,  $\hat{\mathcal{f}}_0 := \bar{f} + \left(2^{13/4} + 15 + \frac{19}{40}\right)\frac{\hat{\sigma}_x^2}{\bar{\zeta}}$  with  $\bar{f} := \max_{(x,y) \in \mathcal{x} \times \mathcal{y}} f(x,y)$ .

proof by  $\bar{\zeta} \leq \frac{1}{10l_{11}}, q_k = \frac{19}{20\zeta k^{1/4}}, \forall k \geq 1$ , let us denote  $\bar{\gamma}_k = \bar{\zeta} l_{21}^2 + \frac{16\tau l_{21}^2}{\bar{\zeta}(q_{k+1})^2} - 2\bar{\nu}$ ,  $p_k = \frac{8(\tau-2)l_{21}^2}{\bar{\zeta}(q_{k+1})^2}$ , it can be easily checked that the relations in (4.36) are satisfied. it follows from the selection of  $\bar{\gamma}_k$  and  $p_k$  that

$$
\bar {\nu} + \frac {\bar {\gamma} _ {k}}{2} - \frac {\bar {\zeta} l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {1 6 l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{\bar {\zeta} (q _ {k + 1}) ^ {2}} = p _ {k}.
$$

this observation, in view of lemma 4.4, then immediately implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} p _ {k} \| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \frac {9}{1 0 \bar {\zeta}} \| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \| ^ {2} \leq \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {k + 1} - \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {k} + \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta}} \left(\frac {q _ {k - 1}}{q _ {k}} - \frac {q _ {k}}{q _ {k + 1}}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} \\ + \frac {q _ {k - 1} - q _ {k}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.40} \\ \end{array}
$$

we can easily check from the definition of  $f_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})$  that

$$
\left\| \nabla g _ {k} \right\| - \left\| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \right\| \leq q _ {k} \| x _ {k} \|.
$$

similar to (4.22) in the proof of theorem 4.1, by replacing  $f$  with  $f_{k}$ ,  $\nabla g_{k}$  with  $\nabla \tilde{g}_k$ ,  $\zeta$  with  $\bar{\zeta}$ ,  $\nu$  with  $\bar{\gamma}_k + \bar{\nu}$  respectively, we conclude that

$$
\left\| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} \leq 2 \left(\bar {\gamma} _ {k} + \bar {\nu}\right) ^ {2} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} + \left(\frac {1}{\bar {\zeta} ^ {2}} + 2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}\right) \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {4.41}
$$

since both  $p_k$  and  $\bar{\gamma}_k$  are in the same order when  $k$  becomes large enough, it then follows from the definition of  $\hat{d}_1$  that  $\forall k \geq 1$ ,

$$
\hat {d} _ {1} \geq \frac {2 \left(\bar {\gamma} _ {k} + \bar {\nu}\right) ^ {2}}{\left(p _ {k}\right) ^ {2}}. \tag {4.42}
$$

combining the previous two inequalities in (4.42) and (4.41), we obtain

$$
\left\| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} \leq \hat {d} _ {1} \left(p _ {k}\right) ^ {2} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} + \left(\frac {1}{\bar {\zeta} ^ {2}} + 2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}\right) \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {4.43}
$$

denote  $\hat{d}_k^{(2)} = \frac{1}{\max\left\{\hat{d}_1p_k,\frac{10 + 20\bar{\zeta}^2l_{12}^2}{9\zeta}\right\}}$  . by multiplying  $\hat{d}_k^{(2)}$  on the both sides of (4.43), and using (4.40), we get

$$
\hat {d} _ {k} ^ {(2)} \| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \leq \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {k + 1} - \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {k} + \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta}} \left(\frac {q _ {k - 1}}{q _ {k}} - \frac {q _ {k}}{q _ {k + 1}}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2} + \frac {q _ {k - 1} - q _ {k}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} \| ^ {2}. \tag {4.44}
$$

denote  $\hat{t}(\varepsilon) \coloneqq \min \{k \mid \| \nabla \tilde{g}(x_k, y_k) \| \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2}, k \geq 2\}$ . by summing both sides of (4.44) from  $k = 2$  to  $k = \hat{t}(\varepsilon)$ , we then obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\hat {t} (\varepsilon)} \hat {d} _ {k} ^ {(2)} \| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {\hat {t} (\varepsilon) + 1} - \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {2} + \frac {8}{\bar {\zeta}} \left(\frac {q _ {1}}{q _ {2}} - \frac {q _ {\hat {t} (\varepsilon)}}{q _ {\hat {t} (\varepsilon) + 1}}\right) \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2} + \frac {q _ {1} - q _ {\hat {t} (\varepsilon)}}{2} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2} \\ \leq \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {\hat {t} (\varepsilon) + 1} - \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {2} + \frac {8 q _ {1}}{\bar {\zeta} q _ {2}} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2} + \frac {q _ {1}}{2} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2} \\ = \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {\hat {t} (\varepsilon) + 1} - \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {2} + \frac {8 \cdot 2 ^ {1 / 4}}{\bar {\zeta}} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2} + \frac {1 9}{4 0 \bar {\zeta}} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2}. \tag {4.45} \\ \end{array}
$$

note that by the definition of  $\hat{\mathcal{f}}_{k + 1}$  in lemma 4.4, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {\hat {t} (\varepsilon) + 1} \leq \bar {f} + \frac {8 \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2}}{\bar {\zeta}} \left(1 + \frac {1}{\hat {t} (\varepsilon)}\right) ^ {1 / 4} + \frac {1 5 \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2}}{\bar {\zeta}} + \frac {q _ {1} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2}}{2} \\ = \bar {f} + \left(2 ^ {1 3 / 4} + 1 5 + \frac {1 9}{4 0}\right) \frac {\hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2}}{\bar {\zeta}} = \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {0}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\bar{f} := \max_{(x,y) \in \mathcal{x} \times \mathcal{y}} f(x,y)$ . we then conclude from (4.45) that

$$
\sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\hat {t} (\varepsilon)} \hat {d} _ {k} ^ {(2)} \| \nabla \tilde {g} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \leq \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {0} - \hat {\mathcal {f}} _ {2} + \frac {8 \cdot 2 ^ {1 / 4}}{\bar {\zeta}} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2} + \frac {1 9}{4 0 \bar {\zeta}} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {2} = \hat {d} _ {3}. \tag {4.46}
$$

note that  $\hat{d}_4 = \max \{\hat{d}_1, \frac{10 + 20\bar{\zeta}^2l_{12}^2}{9\bar{\zeta}p_2}\}$ . since  $p_k$  is an increasing sequence when  $k \geq 2$ , we have that  $\hat{d}_4 \geq \max \{\hat{d}_1, \frac{10 + 20\bar{\zeta}^2l_{12}^2}{9\bar{\zeta}p_k}\}$ , which implies that  $\hat{d}_k^{(2)} \geq \frac{1}{\hat{d}_4p_k}$ . by multiplying  $\hat{d}_4$  on the both sides of (4.46), and using the definition of  $\hat{d}_3$ , we have  $\sum_{k=2}^{\hat{t}(\varepsilon)} \frac{1}{p_k} \| \nabla \tilde{g}_k \|^2 \leq \hat{d}_3 \hat{d}_4$ , which, by the definition of  $\hat{t}(\varepsilon)$ , implies that

$$
\frac {\varepsilon^ {2}}{4} \leq \frac {\hat {d} _ {3} \hat {d} _ {4}}{\sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\hat {t} (\varepsilon)} \frac {1}{p _ {k}}}. \tag {4.47}
$$

using the assumptions  $q_{k} = \frac{19}{20\zeta k^{1 / 4}}$  and  $p_k = \frac{2\cdot 40^2\bar{\zeta}(\tau - 2)l_{21}^2\sqrt{k + 1}}{19^2}$ , (4.47) and the fact  $\sum_{k = 2}^{\hat{t} (\varepsilon)}1 / \sqrt{k + 1}\geq \sqrt{\hat{t}(\varepsilon)} -2$ , we conclude that  $\frac{\varepsilon^2}{4}\leq \frac{2\cdot 40^2\bar{\zeta}(\tau - 2)l_{21}^2\hat{d}_3\hat{d}_4}{19^2\left(\sqrt{\hat{t}(\varepsilon)} - 2\right)}$  or equivalently,

$$
\hat {t} (\varepsilon) \leq \left(\frac {2 \cdot 8 0 ^ {2} \bar {\zeta} (\tau - 2) l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \hat {d} _ {3} \hat {d} _ {4}}{1 9 ^ {2} \varepsilon^ {2}} + 2\right) ^ {2}.
$$

on the other hand, if  $k > \frac{19^4\hat{\sigma}_x^4}{10^4\zeta^4\varepsilon^4}$ , then  $q_{k} = \frac{19}{20\hat{\zeta}k^{1 / 4}} \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2\hat{\sigma}_{x}}$ , this inequality together with the definition of  $\hat{\sigma}_x$  then imply that  $q_{k}\| x_{k}\| \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ . therefore, there exists a

$$
t (\varepsilon) \leq \max (\hat {t} (\varepsilon), \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {4}}{1 0 ^ {4} \bar {\zeta} ^ {4} \varepsilon^ {4}}) \leq \max \left(\left(\frac {2 \cdot 8 0 ^ {2} \bar {\zeta} (\tau - 2) l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \hat {d} _ {3} \hat {d} _ {4}}{1 9 ^ {2} \varepsilon^ {2}} + 2\right) ^ {2}, \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \hat {\sigma} _ {x} ^ {4}}{1 0 ^ {4} \bar {\zeta} ^ {4} \varepsilon^ {4}}\right),
$$

such that  $\| \nabla g_k\| \leq \| \nabla \tilde{g}_k\| +q_k\| x_k\| \leq \frac{\varepsilon}{2} +\frac{\varepsilon}{2} = \varepsilon$

theorem 4.2 shows that the number of gradient evaluations performed by algorithm 1 to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point of  $f$  which satisfies (4.36) is bounded by  $\mathcal{o}\left(l^4\varepsilon^{-4}\right)$  under the general convex-nonconcave setting. to the best of our knowledge, this is the first theoretical guarantee that has been obtained in the literature for solving this class of minimax problems. as mentioned in section 1, it is difficult to extend existing algorithms, especially those nested-loop methods, for minimax optimization to the general convex-nonconcave setting. one possible approach would be to switch the order of the "min" and "max" operators. however, in general,  $\min_{x\in \mathcal{x}}\max_{y\in \mathcal{y}}f(x,y)\neq \max_{y\in \mathcal{y}}\min_{x\in \mathcal{x}}f(x,y)$  when  $f(x,y)$  is nonconvex w.r.t.  $x$  or nonconcave w.r.t.  $y$ . even if the set of stationary points for the above two problems are the same, e.g., by using the same stationarity criterion as in this paper, the algorithm applied to these problems will converge to different solutions. this can be seen by applying the unified agp algorithm to solve  $\min_{x\in \mathcal{x}}\max_{y\in \mathcal{y}}f(x,y)$  and  $\max_{y\in \mathcal{y}}\min_{x\in \mathcal{x}}f(x,y)$ . the agp algorithm will likely converge to different stationary points because different potential functions (i.e.,  $\mathcal{f}_{k + 1}$  and  $\hat{\mathcal{f}}_{k + 1}$  in lemma 3.6 and lemma 4.6) have to be applied for analyzing the convergence of agp for solving these two problems.

for nonconvex-strongly concave or strongly convex-nonconcave setting, the agp algorithm with properly chosen parameters is actually equivalent to the alternating gda algorithm. by constructing suitable potential function, i.e.,  $f_{k + 1}$ , we were able to show the  $o(\epsilon^{-2})$  iteration complexity of this method. whereas for nonconvex-concave or convex-nonconcave setting, where  $b_{k}$  or  $c_{k}$  is not equal to 0, agp algorithm is completely new and the selection of these parameters  $b_{k}$  or  $c_{k}$  plays a very crucial role to guarantee the convergence of the apg method. in these cases, the key step in our proof is also to construct a suitable potential function, i.e.,  $\mathcal{f}_{k + 1}$  and  $\hat{\mathcal{f}}_{k + 1}$  with  $b_{k}$  or  $c_{k}$  playing a very crucial role (see lemma 3.6 and lemma 4.6 respectively). to our best knowledge, this is the first time that such potential functions have been constructed for solving minimax problems.

# 5 block-wise nonsmooth nonconvex minimax problems

in this section, we consider a more general block-wise nonsmooth minimax problem as follows.

$$
\min  _ {x \in \mathcal {x}} \max  _ {y \in \mathcal {y}} l (x, y) := f \left(x ^ {(1)}, x ^ {(2)}, \dots , x ^ {\left(k _ {1}\right)}, y ^ {(1)}, y ^ {(2)}, \dots , y ^ {\left(k _ {2}\right)}\right) + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k _ {1}} h _ {i} \left(x ^ {(i)}\right) - \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {k _ {2}} g _ {j} \left(y ^ {(j)}\right), \tag {bp}
$$

s.t.  $x^{(i)}\in \mathcal{x}_i,\quad i = 1,\dots ,k_1,$

$$
y ^ {(j)} \in \mathcal {y} _ {j}, \quad j = 1, \dots , k _ {2},
$$

where  $f: \mathbb{r}^{d_xk_1 + d_yk_2} \to \mathbb{r}$  is a continuously differentiable function,  $h_i: \mathbb{r}^{d_x} \to \mathbb{r}$  and  $g_j: \mathbb{r}^{d_y} \to \mathbb{r}$  are some convex continuous possibly nonsmooth functions,  $x = [x^{(1)}, x^{(2)}, \dots, x^{(k_1)}] \in \mathcal{x} = \mathcal{x}_1 \times \mathcal{x}_2 \times \dots \times \mathcal{x}_{k_1} \subset \mathbb{r}^{d_xk_1}$  and  $y = [y^{(1)}, y^{(2)}, \dots, y^{(k_2)}] \in \mathcal{y} = \mathcal{y}_1 \times \mathcal{y}_2 \times \dots \times \mathcal{y}_{k_2} \subset \mathbb{r}^{d_yk_2}$  are the block variables,  $\mathcal{x}_i (i = 1, \dots, k_1)$  and  $\mathcal{y}_j (j = 1, \dots, k_2)$  are nonempty compact convex sets. for notational simplicity, we denote  $f(x^{(1)}, x^{(2)}, \dots, x^{(k_1)}, y^{(1)}, y^{(2)}, \dots, y^{(k_2)})$  by  $f(x, y)$ .

note that (bp) reduces to (p) if we set  $k_{1} = k_{2} = 1$ ,  $h_{i}(\cdot) = 0$  and  $g_{j}(\cdot) = 0$ , which means that (p) is a special case of (bp). there exist very few known existing nested-loop algorithms that are proposed to solve (bp) with multi-block structure. however, the minimax problems with block structure are important in machine learning and signal processing, e.g., distributed training [33].

the difficulty to solve (bp) comes from two aspects. one is that for any given  $j$ ,  $f(x, y^{(1)}, y^{(2)}, \dots, y^{(k_2)})$  is nonconcave w.r.t.  $y^{(j)}$  for any given  $x$  and other blocks of  $y$ , i.e.,  $y^{(1)}, y^{(2)}, \dots, y^{(j-1)}, y^{(j+1)}, \dots, y^{(k_2-1)}$  and  $y^{(k_2)}$ . for any given  $x$ , to solve the inner maximization subproblems with respect to  $y^{(j)}$  is already np-hard. due to this reason, almost all the existing nested-loop algorithms will lose their theoretic guarantees since they need to solve the inner subproblem exactly, or approximately with an error proportional to the accuracy  $\varepsilon$ , unless we

can exchange the order of "min" and "max" operators. however, in general,  $\min_{x \in \mathcal{x}} \max_{y \in \mathcal{y}} l(x, y) \neq \max_{y \in \mathcal{y}} \min_{x \in \mathcal{x}} l(x, y)$  when  $f(x, y)$  is nonconvex w.r.t.  $x$  or nonconcave w.r.t.  $y$ . although  $\min_{x \in \mathcal{x}} \max_{y \in \mathcal{y}} l(x, y)$  and  $\max_{y \in \mathcal{y}} \min_{x \in \mathcal{x}} l(x, y)$  share the same stationary point set, when we use the same algorithm to solve them, it may converge to different stationary points. another difficulty comes from the multi-block structure. to the best of our knowledge, there are very few nested-loop algorithms (and complexity results) for solving the aforementioned multi-block structure nonconvex minimax problems before our work. on the other hand, single loop algorithms do not need to solve the inner subproblem, and can be easily generalized to handle the multi-block structure.

similar to the idea of agp algorithm, we propose a block alternating proximal gradient algorithm (bapg) to solve (bp). instead of the original function  $l(x,y)$ , the bapg algorithm uses the gradient of a regularized version of the original function, i.e.,

$$
\bar {l} _ {k} (x, y) := \bar {f} _ {k} (x, y) + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k _ {1}} h _ {i} \left(x ^ {(i)}\right) - \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {k _ {2}} g _ {j} \left(y ^ {(j)}\right), \tag {5.1}
$$

where  $\bar{f}_k(x,y) = f(x,y) + \frac{\hat{b}_k}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k_1}\|x^{(i)}\|^2 - \frac{\hat{c}_k}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k_2}\|y^{(j)}\|^2$  with regularization parameters  $\hat{b}_k \geq 0$  and  $\hat{c}_k \geq 0$  at the  $k$ th iteration. before presenting the detailed algorithm, we give some notations as follows. we denote two proximity operators for  $x^{(i)}$  and  $y^{(j)}$  as follows,

$$
\operatorname {p r o x} _ {h _ {i}, \mathcal {x} _ {i}} ^ {\beta^ {(i)}} (v ^ {(i)}) := \arg \min  _ {x ^ {(i)} \in \mathcal {x} _ {i}} h _ {i} (x ^ {(i)}) + \frac {\beta^ {(i)}}{2} \| x ^ {(i)} - v ^ {(i)} \| ^ {2}, \tag {5.2}
$$

$$
\operatorname {p r o x} _ {g _ {j}, \mathcal {y} _ {j}} ^ {\xi^ {(j)}} \left(\omega^ {(j)}\right) := \arg \max  _ {y ^ {(j)} \in \mathcal {y} _ {j}} - g _ {j} \left(y ^ {(j)}\right) - \frac {\xi^ {(j)}}{2} \left\| y ^ {(j)} - \omega^ {(j)} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {5.3}
$$

let  $k$  be the number of iteration. denote  $x_{k} = \left[x_{k}^{(1)},x_{k}^{(2)},\dots ,x_{k}^{(k_{1})}\right]$ ,  $y_{k} = \left[y_{k}^{(1)},y_{k}^{(2)},\dots ,y_{k}^{(k_{2})}\right]$  and define

$$
v _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} := \left[ x _ {k + 1} ^ {(1)}, x _ {k + 1} ^ {(2)}, \dots , x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i - 1)}, x _ {k} ^ {(i)}, \dots x _ {k} ^ {(k _ {1})} \right],
$$

$$
v _ {k + 1} ^ {(- i)} := \left[ x _ {k + 1} ^ {(1)}, x _ {k + 1} ^ {(2)}, \dots , x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i - 1)}, x _ {k} ^ {(i + 1)}, \dots x _ {k} ^ {(k _ {1})} \right],
$$

$$
w _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)} := \left[ y _ {k + 1} ^ {(1)}, y _ {k + 1} ^ {(2)}, \dots , y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j - 1)}, y _ {k} ^ {(j)}, \dots y _ {k} ^ {(k _ {2})} \right],
$$

$$
w _ {k + 1} ^ {(- j)} := \left[ y _ {k + 1} ^ {(1)}, y _ {k + 1} ^ {(2)}, \dots , y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j - 1)}, y _ {k} ^ {(j + 1)}, \dots y _ {k} ^ {(k _ {2})} \right].
$$

each iteration of the proposed bapg algorithm conducts two proximal gradient steps for updating both  $x$  and  $y$ . more specifically, at the  $k$ th iteration, it updates  $x_{k}$  by minimizing a linearized approximation of  $\bar{l}_k(x,y_k)$  with the gradient at point  $\left(v_{k + 1}^{(i)},y_k\right)$ , i.e., for each  $i = 1,\dots ,k_1$

$$
\begin{array}{l} x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} = \arg \min _ {x ^ {(i)} \in \mathcal {x} _ {i}} \left\langle \nabla_ {x ^ {(i)}} \bar {f} _ {k} (v _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)}, y _ {k}), x ^ {(i)} - x _ {k} ^ {(i)} \right\rangle + h _ {i} (x ^ {(i)}) + \frac {\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)}}{2} \| x ^ {(i)} - x _ {k} ^ {(i)} \| ^ {2} \\ = \mathrm {p r o x} _ {h _ {i}, \mathcal {x} _ {i}} ^ {\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)}} \left(x _ {k} ^ {(i)} - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)}} \nabla_ {x ^ {(i)}} f (v _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)}, y _ {k}) - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)}} \hat {b} _ {k} x _ {k} ^ {(i)}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\mathrm{prox}_{h_i,\mathcal{x}_i}^{\beta_k^{(i)}}$  is the proximal operator which is defined in (5.2) and  $\beta_{k}^{(i)} > 0$ . similarly, it updates  $y_{k}$  by maximizing a linearized approximation of  $\bar{l}_k(x_{k + 1},y)$  minus some regularized terms, i.e.,

$$
\begin{array}{l} y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)} = \arg \max _ {y ^ {(j)} \in \mathcal {y} _ {j}} \langle \nabla_ {y ^ {(j)}} \bar {f} _ {k} (x _ {k + 1}, w _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)}), y ^ {(j)} - y _ {k} ^ {(j)} \rangle - g _ {j} (y ^ {(j)}) - \frac {\xi_ {k} ^ {(j)}}{2} \| y ^ {(j)} - y _ {k} ^ {(j)} \| ^ {2} \\ = \mathrm {p r o x} _ {g _ {j}, \mathcal {y} _ {j}} ^ {\xi_ {k} ^ {(j)}} \left(y _ {k} ^ {(j)} + \frac {1}{\xi_ {k} ^ {(j)}} \nabla_ {y ^ {(j)}} f (x _ {k + 1}, w _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)}) - \frac {1}{\xi_ {k} ^ {(j)}} \hat {c} _ {k} y _ {k} ^ {(j)}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\mathrm{prox}_{g_j,\mathcal{y}_j}^{\xi_k^{(j)}}$  is the proximal operator which is defined in (5.3) and  $\xi_{k}^{(j)} > 0$ . the proposed bapg algorithm is formally stated in algorithm 2, where sequences  $\beta_{k}^{(i)},\hat{b}_{k},\xi_{k}^{(j)},\hat{c}_{k}$  and the stopping rule in step 4 will be

# algorithm 2 (bapg algorithm)

step 1: input:  $x_{1}, y_{1}$ ,  $\hat{b}_{1}$ ,  $\hat{c}_{1}$ ,  $\beta_{1}^{(i)}$  ( $i = 1, \dots, k_{1}$ ),  $\xi_{1}^{(j)}$  ( $j = 1, \dots, k_{2}$ ); set  $k = 1$ .  
step 2: calculate  $\hat{b}_k, \beta_k^{(i)}$ , and for  $i = 1, \dots, k_1$ , perform the following update for  $x_k^{(i)}$ :

$$
x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} = \mathrm {p r o x} _ {h _ {i}, \mathcal {x} _ {i}} ^ {\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)}} \left(x _ {k} ^ {(i)} - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)}} \nabla_ {x ^ {(i)}} f (v _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)}, y _ {k}) - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)}} \hat {b} _ {k} x _ {k} ^ {(i)}\right). \tag {5.4}
$$

step 3: calculate  $\hat{c}_k$ ,  $\xi_k^{(j)}$ , and for  $j = 1, \dots, k_2$  and perform the following update for  $y_k^{(j)}$ :

$$
y _ {k + 1} = \operatorname {p r o x} _ {g _ {j}, \mathcal {y} _ {j}} ^ {\xi_ {k} ^ {(j)}} \left(y _ {k} ^ {(j)} + \frac {1}{\xi_ {k} ^ {(j)}} \nabla_ {y ^ {(j)}} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, w _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)}\right) - \frac {1}{\xi_ {k} ^ {(j)}} \hat {c} _ {k} y _ {k} ^ {(j)}\right). \tag {5.5}
$$

step 4: if some stationary condition is satisfied, stop; otherwise, set  $k = k + 1$ , go to step 2.

specified later in each of the different problem settings to be studied. note that it reduces to the agp algorithm when  $k_{1} = k_{2} = 1$ ,  $h_{i}(\cdot) = 0$  and  $g_{j}(\cdot) = 0$ .

before analyzing the convergence of algorithm 2, we define the stationarity gap as the termination criterion as follows.

definition 5.1 at each iteration of algorithm 2, the stationarity gap for problem (bp) w.r.t.  $l(x,y)$  is defined as:

$$
\nabla \mathcal {g} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) := \left[ \begin{array}{c} \beta_ {k} ^ {(1)} \left(x _ {k} ^ {(1)} - \operatorname {p r o x} _ {h _ {1}, \mathcal {x} _ {1}} ^ {\beta_ {k} ^ {(1)}} \left(x _ {k} ^ {(1)} - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k} ^ {(1)}} \nabla_ {x ^ {(1)}} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k})\right)\right) \\ \vdots \\ \beta_ {k} ^ {(k _ {1})} \left(x _ {k} ^ {(k _ {1})} - \operatorname {p r o x} _ {h _ {k _ {1}}, \mathcal {x} _ {k _ {1}}} ^ {\beta_ {k} ^ {(k _ {1})}} \left(x _ {k} ^ {(k _ {1})} - \frac {1}{\beta_ {k} ^ {(k _ {1})}} \nabla_ {x ^ {(k _ {1})}} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k})\right)\right) \\ \xi_ {k} ^ {(1)} \left(y _ {k} ^ {(1)} - \operatorname {p r o x} _ {g _ {1}, \mathcal {y} _ {1}} ^ {\xi_ {k} ^ {(1)}} \left(y _ {k} ^ {(1)} + \frac {1}{\xi_ {k} ^ {(1)}} \nabla_ {y ^ {(1)}} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k})\right)\right) \\ \vdots \\ \xi_ {k} ^ {(k _ {2})} \left(y _ {k} ^ {(k _ {2})} - \operatorname {p r o x} _ {g _ {k _ {2}}, \mathcal {y} _ {k _ {2}}} ^ {\xi_ {k} ^ {(k _ {2})}} \left(y _ {k} ^ {(k _ {2})} + \frac {1}{\xi_ {k} ^ {(k _ {2})}} \nabla_ {y ^ {(k _ {2})}} f (x _ {k}, y _ {k})\right)\right) \end{array} \right].
$$

for simplicity, we denote  $\nabla \mathcal{g}_k\coloneqq \nabla \mathcal{g}(x_k,y_k)$ $(\nabla \mathcal{g}_k)_{x^{(i)}}\coloneqq \beta_k^{(i)}(x_k^{(i)} - \mathrm{prox}_{h_i,\mathcal{x}_i}^{\beta_k^{(i)}}(x_k^{(i)} - \frac{1}{\beta_k^{(i)}}\nabla_{x^{(i)}}f(x_k,y_k)))$  and  $(\nabla \mathcal{g}_k)_{y^{(j)}}\coloneqq \xi_k^{(j)}\left(y_k^{(j)} - \mathrm{prox}_{g_j,\mathcal{y}_j}^{\xi_k^{(j)}}(y_k^{(j)} + \frac{1}{\xi_k^{(j)}}\nabla_{y^{(j)}}f(x_k,y_k))\right).$

definition 5.2 at each iteration of algorithm 2, the stationarity gap for problem (bp) w.r.t.  $\bar{l}_k(x,y)$ , denoted by  $\nabla \bar{\mathcal{g}}(x_k,y_k)$ , is defined almost the same as in definition 5.1 except by replacing  $f$  with  $\bar{f}_k$ . for simplicity, we denote  $\nabla \bar{\mathcal{g}}_k \coloneqq \nabla \bar{\mathcal{g}}(x_k,y_k)$ ,  $(\nabla \bar{\mathcal{g}}_k)_{x^{(i)}} \coloneqq \beta_k^{(i)}(x_k^{(i)} - \mathrm{prox}_{h_i,\mathcal{x}_i}^{\beta_k^{(i)}}(x_k^{(i)} - \frac{1}{\beta_k^{(i)}}\nabla_{x^{(i)}}\bar{f}_k(x_k,y_k)))$  and  $(\nabla \bar{\mathcal{g}}_k)_{y^{(j)}} \coloneqq \xi_k^{(j)}\left(y_k^{(j)} - \mathrm{prox}_{g_j,\mathcal{y}_j}^{\xi_k^{(j)}}(y_k^{(j)} + \frac{1}{\xi_k^{(j)}}\nabla_{y^{(j)}}\bar{f}_k(x_k,y_k))\right)$ .

# 5.1 complexity analysis for nonconvex-(strongly) concave setting

# 5.1.1 nonconvex-strongly concave setting

in this subsection, we analyze the iteration complexity of algorithm 2 for solving the nonsmooth one-sided blockwise nonconvex-strongly concave minimax optimization problem (bp) with  $k_{2} = 1$ , i.e.,  $f(x,y)$  is nonconvex w.r.t.  $x$  for any fixed  $y \in \mathcal{v}$ , and  $\hat{\mu}$ -strongly concave w.r.t.  $y$  for any given  $x \in \mathcal{x}$ . under this setting,  $\forall k \geq 1$ , we set

$$
\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)} = \hat {\eta}, \xi_ {k} ^ {(j)} = \frac {1}{\hat {\rho}}, \text {a n d} \hat {b} _ {k} = \hat {c} _ {k} = 0. \tag {5.6}
$$

lemma 5.1 below shows a descent result for the  $x_{k}$  update.

lemma 5.1 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 2 with parameter settings in (5.6). if  $\forall k, \hat{\eta} > l_{11}$ , we have

$$
l \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - l \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \leq - \frac {\hat {\eta}}{2} \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {5.7}
$$

proof by the optimality condition for (5.4),  $\forall i$  we have

$$
\langle \nabla_ {x ^ {(i)}} f (v _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)}, y _ {k}) + \partial h _ {i} (x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)}) + \hat {\eta} (x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} - x _ {k} ^ {(i)}), x _ {k} ^ {(i)} - x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} \rangle \geq 0. \tag {5.8}
$$

by assumption 2.1 and the convexity of  $h_i(x)$ , we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} l (x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)}, v _ {k + 1} ^ {(- i)}, y _ {k}) - l (x _ {k} ^ {(i)}, v _ {k + 1} ^ {(- i)}, y _ {k}) \\ \leq \left\langle \nabla_ {x ^ {(i)}} f \left(v _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)}, y _ {k}\right) + \partial h _ {i} \left(x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)}\right), x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} - x _ {k} ^ {(i)} \right\rangle + \frac {l _ {1 1}}{2} \| x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} - x _ {k} ^ {(i)} \| ^ {2}. \tag {5.9} \\ \end{array}
$$

adding (5.8) and (5.9), and summing up the inequality from  $i = 1$  to  $i = k_{1}$ , we have

$$
l (x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}) - l \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \leq - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k _ {1}} \left(\hat {\eta} - \frac {l _ {1 1}}{2}\right) \| x _ {k + 1} ^ {(i)} - x _ {k} ^ {(i)} \| ^ {2}.
$$

by using the assumption that  $\hat{\eta} > l_{11}$ , we complete the proof.

the rest of the proof is almost the same as that of the agp algorithm shown in section 3 since the ascent step of  $y$ 's update can be similarly estimated. by replacing  $\nabla_y f(x_{k+1}, y_k)$  with  $\nabla_y f(x_{k+1}, y_k) - \partial g_1(y_{k+1})$ , and using  $\langle \partial g_1(y_{k+1}) - \partial g_1(y_k), y_{k+1} - y_k \rangle \geq 0$ , and the fact that  $\| x_{k+1}^{(i)} - x_k^{(i)}\| \leq \| x_{k+1} - x_k\|$  and  $\| v_{k+1}^{(i)} - x_k\| \leq \| x_{k+1} - x_k\|$ , we can show similar results to those in lemmas 3.1 and 3.2. then, similar to the proof of theorem 3.1, we obtain the following convergence result by some parameters replacement, e.g.,  $\eta$  with  $\hat{\eta} + l_{11}$  in (3.23) and  $\eta^2$  with  $k_1(\hat{\eta} + l_{11})^2$  in (3.25). we omit the proof details here for simplicity.

in particular, letting  $\nabla \mathcal{g}(x_k,y_k)$  be defined as in definition 5.1 and  $\varepsilon >0$  be a given target accuracy, we provide a bound on  $\mathcal{t}(\varepsilon)$ , the first iteration index to achieve an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point, i.e.,  $\| \nabla \mathcal{g}(x_k,y_k)\| \leq \varepsilon$ , which is equivalent to

$$
\mathcal {t} (\varepsilon) := \min  \left\{k \mid \| \nabla \mathcal {g} \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \| \leq \varepsilon \right\}. \tag {5.10}
$$

theorem 5.1 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 2 with parameter settings in (5.6). if

$$
\hat {\eta} > l _ {1 1}, \hat {\eta} > l _ {1 2} ^ {2} \hat {\rho} + \frac {4 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}}{\hat {\rho} \hat {\mu} ^ {2}}, \text {a n d} \hat {\rho} \leq \frac {\hat {\mu}}{4 l _ {2 2} ^ {2}},
$$

then it holds that

$$
\mathcal {t} \left(\varepsilon\right) \leq \frac {l _ {1} - \underline {{l}}}{r _ {1} \varepsilon^ {2}},
$$

where  $r_1 \coloneqq \frac{\min\left\{\frac{\hat{\eta}}{2} - \frac{\hat{\rho}l_{12}^2}{2} - \frac{2l_{12}^2}{\hat{\rho}\hat{\mu}^2}, \frac{3\hat{\mu} - \hat{\rho}l_{22}^2}{2} + \frac{\hat{\mu} - 4\hat{\rho}l_{22}^2}{2\hat{\rho}\hat{\mu}}\right\}}{\max\left\{k_1(\hat{\eta} + l_{11})^2 + 2l_{12}^2, \frac{2}{\hat{\rho}^2}\right\}}$ ,  $l_1 \coloneqq l(x_1, y_1) + \frac{2\delta_y^2}{\hat{\rho}^2\hat{\mu}}$  and  $\underline{l} \coloneqq \underline{l} - (\hat{\mu} + \frac{7}{2\hat{\rho}} - \frac{\hat{\rho}l_{22}^2}{2} - \frac{2l_{22}^2}{\hat{\mu}})\delta_y^2$  with  $\underline{l} \coloneqq \min_{(x,y) \in \mathcal{x} \times \mathcal{y}} l(x,y)$  and  $\delta_y \coloneqq \max\{\|y_1 - y_2\| \mid y_1, y_2 \in \mathcal{v}\}$ .

theorem 5.1 implies that the iteration complexity of algorithm 2 to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point for solving general block-wise nonsmooth nonconvex-strongly concave minimax problems (bp) is bounded by  $\mathcal{o}\left(l^2\varepsilon^{-2}\right)$ .

# 5.1.2 nonconvex-concave setting

we analyze the iteration complexity of algorithm 2 for solving (bp) in the nonconvex-concave setting. under this setting, let  $k_{2} = 1$ , and  $\forall k \geq 1$ , we set

$$
\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)} = \iota + \vartheta_ {k}, \quad \xi_ {k} ^ {(j)} = \frac {1}{\xi}, \text {a n d} \hat {b} _ {k} = 0, \tag {5.11}
$$

where  $\vartheta_{k}$  is stepsize parameter to be defined later.

assumption 5.1  $\{\hat{c}_k\}$  is a nonnegative monotonically decreasing sequence.

lemma 5.2 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 2 with parameter settings in (5.11). if  $\forall k, \vartheta_k > l_{11}$ , we have

$$
l \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) - l \left(x _ {k}, y _ {k}\right) \leq - \left(\iota + \frac {\vartheta_ {k}}{2}\right) \left\| x _ {k + 1} - x _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {5.12}
$$

proof the proof is the same with that of lemma 5.1 except replacing  $\beta_k^{(i)}$  by  $\iota +\vartheta_{k}$ . we omit the details here.

the rest of the proof is almost the same as that of the agp algorithm shown in section 3 since the ascent step of  $y$ 's update can be similarly estimated. by replacing  $\nabla_y f_k(x_{k + 1},y_k)$  with  $\nabla_y\bar{f}_k(x_{k + 1},y_k) - \partial g_1(y_{k + 1})$ , and using  $\langle \partial g_1(y_{k + 1}) - \partial g_1(y_k),y_{k + 1} - y_k\rangle \geq 0$ , and the fact that  $\| x_{k + 1}^{(i)} - x_k^{(i)}\| \leq \| x_{k + 1} - x_k\|$  and  $\| v_{k + 1}^{(i)} - x_k\| \leq \| x_{k + 1} - x_k\|$ , we can prove similar results to those in lemmas 3.3 and 3.4. then, similar to the proof of theorem 3.2, we obtain the following result only through some parameters replacement, e.g.,  $\bar{\eta} +\bar{\beta}_k$  with  $\iota +\vartheta_{k} + l_{11}$  in (3.49) and  $(\bar{\eta} +\bar{\beta}_k)^2$  with  $k_{1}\left(\iota +\vartheta_{k} + l_{11}\right)^{2}$  in (3.51).

theorem 5.2 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 2 with parameter settings in (5.11). if  $\xi \leq \frac{1}{10l_{22}}$ ,  $\hat{c}_k = \frac{19}{20\xi k^{1/4}}$ ,  $k \geq 1$ ,  $\tau > \max \{\frac{\xi\hat{c}_1^2(l_{11} + 2\iota - \xi l_{12}^2)}{16l_{12}^2}, 2\}$ , then for any given  $\varepsilon > 0$ ,

$$
\mathcal {t} (\varepsilon) \leq \max \left(\left(\frac {2 \cdot 8 0 ^ {2} \xi (\tau - 2) l _ {1 2} ^ {2} r _ {2} r _ {3}}{1 9 ^ {2} \varepsilon^ {2}} + 1\right) ^ {2}, \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \hat {\delta} _ {y} ^ {4}}{1 0 ^ {4} \xi^ {4} \varepsilon^ {4}}\right),
$$

$$
\text {w h e r e} r _ {2} = \mathcal {l} _ {2} - \underline {{\mathcal {l}}} + (8 \cdot 2 ^ {1 / 4} + \frac {1 9}{4 0}) \frac {\hat {\delta} _ {y} ^ {2}}{\xi}, r _ {1} = \frac {8 k _ {1} \tau^ {2}}{(\tau - 2) ^ {2}} + \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \left(2 k _ {1} \left(\xi l _ {1 2} ^ {2} - \iota + l _ {1 1}\right) ^ {2} + 2 l _ {1 2} ^ {2}\right)}{6 4 \cdot 2 0 ^ {4} \xi^ {2} (\tau - 2) ^ {2} l _ {1 2} ^ {4}}, r _ {3} = \max  \{r _ {1}, \frac {1 9 ^ {2}}{1 4 4 0 \sqrt {2} (\tau - 2) \xi^ {2} l _ {1 2} ^ {2}} \},
$$

$$
\hat {\delta} _ {y} := \max  \{\| y \| \mid y \in \mathcal {y} \},   \mathcal {l} _ {2} := l (x _ {2}, y _ {2}) + \frac {1 6 \hat {\delta} _ {y} ^ {2}}{\xi^ {2} \hat {c c c c} _ {2}},   \underline {{\mathcal {l}}} := \underline {{l}} - \left(2 ^ {1 3 / 4} + 1 5 + \frac {1 9}{4 0}\right) \frac {\hat {\delta} _ {y} ^ {2}}{\xi} \text {w i t h} \underline {{l}} := \min  _ {(x, y) \in \mathcal {x} \times \mathcal {y}} l (x, y).
$$

by setting  $\xi = \frac{1}{10l_{22}}$ , from theorem 5.2, we conclude that for any given  $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$

$$
\mathcal {t} (\varepsilon) \leq \max \left(\left(\frac {1 2 8 0 (\tau - 2) l _ {1 2} ^ {2} r _ {2} r _ {3}}{1 9 ^ {2} l _ {2 2} \varepsilon^ {2}} + 1\right) ^ {2}, \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \hat {\delta} _ {y} ^ {4} l _ {2 2} ^ {4}}{\varepsilon^ {4}}\right) = \mathcal {o} \left(l ^ {4} \varepsilon^ {- 4}\right).
$$

this implies that the iteration complexity of the proposed bapg algorithm to obtain a point that satisfies  $\| \nabla \mathcal{g}(x_k,y_k)\| \leq \varepsilon$  with  $\nabla \mathcal{g}(x_k,y_k)$  being defined in definition 5.1 for nonsmooth block-wise nonconvex-concave minimax problems (bp) is bounded by  $\mathcal{o}\left(l^4\varepsilon^{-4}\right)$ .

# 5.2 complexity analysis for (strongly) convex-nonconcave setting

in this subsection we analyze the iteration complexity of algorithm 2 for solving (bp) with  $k_{1} = 1$  under the convex-nonconcave setting.

we first consider the strongly convex-nonconcave setting. under this setting,  $\forall k\geq 1$  , we set

$$
\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)} = \frac {1}{\varrho}, \xi_ {k} ^ {(j)} = \bar {\xi}, \hat {b} _ {k} = \hat {c} _ {k} = 0, \tag {5.13}
$$

lemma 5.3 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 2 with parameter settings in (5.13). if  $\forall k, \bar{\xi} > l_{22}$ , then we have

$$
l \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - l \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) \geq \frac {\bar {\xi}}{2} \left\| y _ {k + 1} - y _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}. \tag {5.14}
$$

proof by the optimality condition for (5.5),  $\forall j$ , we have

$$
\left\langle \nabla_ {y ^ {(j)}} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, w _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)}\right) - \partial g _ {j} \left(y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)}\right) - \bar {\xi} \left(y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)} - y _ {k} ^ {(j)}\right), y _ {k} ^ {(j)} - y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)} \right\rangle \leq 0. \tag {5.15}
$$

by the convexity of  $g_{j}(\cdot)$  and assumption 2.1, the gradient of  $f$  is lipschitz continuous, implying that

$$
\begin{array}{l} l \left(x _ {k + 1}, w _ {k + 1} ^ {(- j)}, y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)}\right) - l \left(x _ {k + 1}, w _ {k + 1} ^ {(- j)}, y _ {k} ^ {(j)}\right) \\ \geq \left\langle \nabla_ {y ^ {(j)}} f \left(x _ {k + 1}, w _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)}\right) - \partial g _ {j} \left(y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)}\right), y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)} - y _ {k} ^ {(j)} \right\rangle - \frac {l _ {2 2}}{2} \| y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)} - y _ {k} ^ {(j)} \| ^ {2}. \tag {5.16} \\ \end{array}
$$

adding (5.15) and (5.16), and then summing it up from  $j = 1$  to  $k_{2}$ , we have

$$
l \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k + 1}\right) - l \left(x _ {k + 1}, y _ {k}\right) \geq \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {k _ {2}} \left(\bar {\xi} - \frac {l _ {2 2}}{2}\right) \left\| y _ {k + 1} ^ {(j)} - y _ {k} ^ {(j)} \right\| ^ {2}.
$$

the result then follows by the assumption that  $\bar{\xi} > l_{22}$ .

by lemma 5.3, similar to the proof of lemma 4.1-4.2 and theorem 4.1 in subsection 4.1, we can prove the following theorem and we omit the details here.

theorem 5.3 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 2 with parameter settings in (5.13). if

$$
\bar {\xi} > l _ {2 2}, \bar {\xi} > l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \varrho + \frac {4 l _ {2 1} ^ {2}}{\varrho \hat {\theta} ^ {2}}, \text {a n d} \varrho \leq \frac {\hat {\theta}}{4 l _ {1 1} ^ {2}},
$$

then  $\forall \varepsilon > 0$ , it holds that

$$
\mathcal {t} \left(\varepsilon\right) \leq \frac {\overline {{l}} - \hat {l} _ {1}}{\hat {r} _ {1} \varepsilon^ {2}},
$$

where  $\hat{r}_1 \coloneqq \frac{\min\left\{\frac{\bar{\xi}}{2} - \frac{\varrho l_{21}^2}{2} - \frac{2l_{21}^2}{\varrho\hat{\theta}^2}, \frac{3\hat{\theta} - \varrho l_{11}^2}{2} + \frac{\hat{\theta} - 4\varrho l_{11}^2}{2\varrho\hat{\theta}}\right\}}{\max\left\{\frac{1}{\varrho^2} + 2k_2l_{12}^2, 2k_2(\bar{\xi} + l_{22})^2\right\}}$  and  $\overline{l} = \hat{l} + (\hat{\theta} + \frac{7}{2\varrho} - \frac{\varrho l_{11}^2}{2} - \frac{2l_{11}^2}{\hat{\theta}}) \delta_x^2$ ,  $\hat{l}_1 \coloneqq \hat{l} - \frac{4 \delta_x^2}{\varrho^2 \hat{\theta}} - \left( \frac{l_{21} \varrho}{2} + \frac{2l_{21}^2}{\hat{\theta}^2 \varrho} \right)$ ,  $\| y_2 - y_1 \|^2$  with  $\hat{l} \coloneqq \max_{(x,y) \in \mathcal{x} \times \mathcal{y}} l(x,y)$  and  $\delta_x = \max \{ \| x_1 - x_2 \| \mid \forall x_1, x_2 \in \mathcal{x} \}$ .

theorem 5.3 implies that the iteration complexity of the proposed bapg algorithm to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point for smooth strongly convex-nonconcave minimax problems (bp) is bounded by  $\mathcal{o}\left(l^2\varepsilon^{-2}\right)$ .

we can also analyze the iteration complexity of algorithm 2 applied to the general convex-nonconcave setting. under this setting,  $\forall k\geq 1$  , we set

$$
\beta_ {k} ^ {(i)} = \frac {1}{\bar {\varrho}}, \quad \xi_ {k} ^ {(j)} = \bar {\iota} + \bar {\vartheta} _ {k}, \quad \hat {c} _ {k} = 0, \tag {5.17}
$$

where  $\xi_k^{(j)}$  is stepsize parameter to be defined later.

assumption 5.2  $\{\hat{b}_k\}$  is a nonnegative monotonically decreasing sequence.

similar to the proof of lemma 4.3-4.4 and theorem 4.2 in subsection 4.2, we can prove the following theorem.

theorem 5.4 suppose that assumption 2.1 holds. let  $\{(x_k, y_k)\}$  be a sequence generated by algorithm 2 with parameter settings in (5.17). if  $\bar{\varrho} \leq \frac{1}{10l_{11}}, \hat{b}_k = \frac{19}{20\bar{\varrho}k^{1/4}}, k \geq 1$ ,  $\tau > \max \left\{\frac{\bar{\varrho}\hat{b}_2^2(l_{22} + 2\bar{\iota} - \bar{\varrho}l_{21}^2)}{16l_{21}^2}, 2\right\}$ , then for any given  $\varepsilon > 0$ ,

$$
\mathcal {t} (\varepsilon) \leq \max \left(\left(\frac {2 \cdot 8 0 ^ {2} \bar {\varrho} (\tau - 2) l _ {2 1} ^ {2} \hat {r} _ {2} \hat {r} _ {3}}{1 9 ^ {2} \varepsilon^ {2}} + 2\right) ^ {2}, \frac {1 9 ^ {4} \hat {\delta} _ {x} ^ {4}}{1 0 ^ {4} \bar {\varrho} ^ {4} \varepsilon^ {4}}\right),
$$

where  $\hat{\delta}_x\coloneqq \max \{\| x\| \mid x\in \mathcal{x}\}$

$$
\hat {r} _ {2} := \hat {\mathcal {l}} _ {0} - \hat {\mathcal {l}} _ {2} + \frac {8 \cdot 2 ^ {1 / 4}}{\bar {\varrho}} \hat {\delta} _ {x} ^ {2} + \frac {1 9}{4 0 \bar {\varrho}} \hat {\delta} _ {x} ^ {2},
$$

$\hat{r}_3 \coloneqq \max \{\hat{d}_1, \frac{10 + 20k_2\bar{\varrho}^2l_{12}^2}{9\bar{\varrho}\alpha_2}\}$  with  $\hat{d}_1 = \frac{16k_2\tau^2}{(\tau - 2)^2} + \frac{19^4\left(k_2\left(\bar{\varrho}l_{21}^2 - \bar{\tau} + l_{22}\right)^2\right)}{16\cdot 20^4\bar{\varrho}^2(\tau - 2)^2l_{21}^4}$ ,  $\hat{\mathcal{l}}_0 \coloneqq \hat{l} + \left(2^{13/4} + 15 + \frac{19}{40}\right)\frac{\hat{\delta}_x^2}{\bar{\varrho}}$  with  $\hat{l} \coloneqq \max_{(x,y) \in \mathcal{x} \times \mathcal{y}} l(x,y)$ ,  $\hat{\mathcal{l}}_2 \coloneqq l(x_2,y_2) - \frac{16\hat{\delta}_x^2}{\bar{\varrho}^2\hat{b}_2} - \frac{8\hat{\delta}_x^2}{\bar{\varrho}} - \left(\frac{\bar{\varrho}l_{21}^2}{2} + \frac{16l_{21}^2}{\bar{\varrho}(\hat{b}_2)^2}\right)\|y_2 - y_1\|^2$ .

theorem 5.4 implies that the iteration complexity of algorithm 2 to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -stationary point for general block-wise nonsmooth convex-nonconcave minimax problems (bp) is bounded by  $\mathcal{o}\left(l^4\varepsilon^{-4}\right)$ .

# 6 numerical results

in this section, we compare the numerical performance of the proposed agp algorithm with the gradient descent ascent algorithm (gda), the alternating gradient descent ascent algorithm (agda) and the state-of-the-art nested-looped algorithm, i.e., minimax-ppa in [32] through two representative test problems. the first numerical test is implemented in python 3.9 and run with an apple m1 processor, while the second one is carried out on an nvidia tesla p100 gpu.

# 6.1 dirac-gan problem

the dirac-gan problem [54] can be formulated as the following nonconvex-concave minimax problem :

$$
\min  _ {x} \max  _ {y} l (x, y) = - \log (1 + \exp (- x y)) + \log 2, \tag {6.1}
$$

where  $(0,0)$  is the unique stationary point.

experimental setup. let  $\alpha_{x}$  and  $\beta_{y}$  be the stepsize of x and y, respectively, and  $t$  denote the outer-loop iteration number for agp, gda, agda and minimax-ppa algorithms. the initial point of all algorithms is chosen as (1,1).

for the agp algorithm, we set  $t = 72$ ,  $\alpha_{x} = \frac{0.8}{\sqrt{k}}$ ,  $\beta_{y} = 0.3$  and  $c_{k} = \frac{0.5}{k^{1/4}}$ . for the gda and agda algorithms, we set  $t = 100$ ,  $\alpha_{x} = 0.3$  and  $\beta_{y} = 0.3$ . for minimax-ppa algorithm, we set  $t = 30$ ,  $\epsilon = 0.01$ ,  $l = 2$ ,  $\mu_{y} = 0.000625$  and  $g(x,y) = l(x,y) - 0.00125 \| y - 1 \|^{2}$ . moreover, for minimax-ppa algorithm, we choose  $\delta = 1e - 6$  and  $\tilde{\epsilon} = 5.8e - 9$  through grid search in the sub-roundines ag2 and agd respectively, since the theoretical settings for these two parameters are  $\delta = 2.38e - 27$  and  $\tilde{\epsilon} = 4.06e - 60$  respectively, which will cause the algorithm to converge very slowly in practice.

results. fig 1 shows the sequences generated by these four algorithms. we find that gda fails to converge, and agda falls into a limit cycle no matter what stepsize is chosen, which is shown in lemma 2.4 in [54]. numerical results show that both agp and minimax-ppa algorithms approach the unique stationary point. note that there are much more parameters needed to be adjusted in advance in minimax-ppa algorithm than the agp algorithm. table 2 shows the total number of iterations and cpu times for both algorithms in solving the dirac-gan problem. we count the total number of iterations in all the sub-roundines in minimax-ppa in [32]. the "distance" column reports the euclidean distance between the point obtained by both algorithms and the unique stationary point. note that the theoretical settings for  $\tilde{\epsilon}$  in the sub-roundine agd in minimax-ppa is actually  $4.06e - 60$  which is almost unacceptable in training a neural network with millions of parameters. in this test, we reduce the precision for the sub-roundine agd in minimax-ppa algorithm in order to obtain a meaningful solution, otherwise it will get stuck. to obtain a solution near  $(0,0)$  with the accuracy within 0.01, minimax-ppa algorithm needs millions number of iterations totally, and hence takes much more cpu time than the agp algorithm.

# 6.2 robust learning over multiple domains

in this subsection, we perform some numerical tests for solving a robust learning problem over multiple domains [46], formulated as a nonconvex-linear minimax problem:

$$
\min  _ {x} \max  _ {y \in \delta} y ^ {t} f (x), \tag {6.2}
$$

where  $f(x) \coloneqq [f_1(x); \ldots; f_m(x)] \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times 1}$  with  $f_m(x) = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{s}_m|} \sum_{i=1}^{|s_m|} \ell(d_i^m, l_i^m, x)$  and  $\ell$  can be any non-negative loss function,  $m$  is the number of tasks and  $x$  denotes the network parameters.  $y \in \delta$  describes the weights over different tasks and  $\delta$  is the simplex, i.e.,  $\delta = \left\{(y_1, \dots, y_m)^t \mid \sum_{m=1}^m y_m = 1, y_m \geq 0\right\}$ . we compare the proposed agp algorithm with the gda algorithm and a heuristic training algorithm with even weights, i.e., fixing  $y_1 = y_2 = 0.5$  when  $m = 2$ , which is also used as a baseline algorithm in many other existing works [33]. we consider two image classification problems with mnist [52] and cifar10 datasets[5]. mnist consists of images about handwritten digits while cifar10 contains 10 different classes such as airplanes, cars, birds etc. our goal is to train a neural network that works on these two completely unrelated problems simultaneously. since cross-entropy loss is popular in multi-class classification problem, we use it as our loss function. we should point out that the quality of the algorithm for solving a robust learning problem over multiple domains is measured by the worst case accuracy over all domains [46].

data augmentation. since the data in mnist are  $28 \times 28$  gray images while those in cifar10 are  $32 \times 32$  rgb images, we first repeat gray channel to rgb channels and resize the examples in mnist from  $28 \times 28$  to  $32 \times 32$  using bilinear interpolation. we adopt alexnet [4] as our base model which is the same as the one used in [33]. then, we convert  $32 \times 32$  to  $224 \times 224$  with the same method to fit the input of alexnet.

experiment setup. we set  $\frac{1}{\beta_k}$ ,  $\gamma_k$ , and  $c_k$  to be  $\frac{2}{2 + \sqrt{k}}$ , 100,  $\frac{1}{10 + k^{\frac{1}{4}}}$  respectively for the agp algorithm. in the gda algorithm, we set  $\alpha = 0.02$  and  $\beta = 0.01$ . in the heuristic algorithm, we set the stepsize of  $x$  to be 0.02, but fix  $y$  as  $(0.5, 0.5)^t$ . we set the batch size in all tasks to be 128 and run 50 epochs for all algorithms. moreover, we sample our results every epoch and calculate the average accuracy on these two testing sets to evaluate the performance of different algorithms.

(a)

![](images/9b3740d939436c0cd504762744f78507e8f231994decb3a6be8dac5184504522.jpg)

fig. 2 performance of three algorithms for solving robust multi-task learning problem.

table 3 comparison the test accuracies for three algorithms on robust multi-task learning

results. figure 2(a) shows the testing accuracy on mnist dataset. all three algorithms achieve high precision, while agp algorithm takes less time than that of gda. figure 2(b) shows the testing accuracy on cifar10 dataset. agp algorithm still performs slightly better than other two algorithms.

table 3 shows the accuracies of all algorithms. mnist and cifar10 indicate training only on mnist and cifar10 dataset respectively. gda and heuristic algorithm can achieve good performance while agp algorithm can further improve the performance and provide a more reliable model for multi-task learning.

remark. due to hardware (especially memory) limitations, we use mini-batch randomized gradient instead of the exact gradient at each iteration in our numerical experiment, which is the same as in [33]. since at each iteration solving the inner subproblem will take huge amounts of time in the nested loop algorithms, e.g., minimax-ppa algorithm, as the gradient calculation needs to go through the whole dataset and they need to calculate gradient several times per iteration. moreover, high requirements for precision in solving subproblems will occupy a lot of memory. hence, we were not able to compare agp algorithm with nested-loop algorithm for this test problem.

# 7 conclusions and discussion

in this paper, we propose a unified single-loop algorithm for general smooth one block nonconvex-concave or convex-nonconcave minimax problems. at each iteration, only one gradient projection step is employed for updating  $x$  and  $y$  respectively. the gradient complexity of the proposed unified agp algorithm under four different settings are established. we prove that an  $\varepsilon$ -first order stationary point of  $f$  can be obtained in  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-4}\right)$  (resp.  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\right)$ ) iterations for nonconvex-concave (resp. nonconvex-strongly concave) setting. to the best of our knowledge, they are the best complexity bounds among single-loop algorithms in general nonconvex-(strongly) concave settings, and very simple to be implemented. numerical results show the efficiency of the proposed agp algorithm. nonetheless, there is still a certain gap between the iteration complexity of the proposed agp algorithm and the best complexity of  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-2.5}\right)$  among nested-looped algorithms in nonconvex-concave setting, which is also an open problem worth studying in the future.

moreover, we consider the (strongly) convex-nonconcave setting of (p). under this setting, the whole problem is convex. however, for any given  $x$ , to solve the inner max subproblem, i.e.,  $\max_{y \in \mathcal{y}} f(x, y)$ , is already np-hard. due to this reason, almost all the existing nested-loop algorithms will lose the theoretic guarantee since they all need to solve the inner subproblem exactly, or in exactly but only an error proportional to the accuracy  $\varepsilon$  is allowed. we show that the proposed unified single-loop agp algorithm can deal with general convex-nonconcave settings. more specifically, the gradient complexity to obtain an  $\varepsilon$ -first-order stationary point of  $f$  is  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\right)$  (resp.,  $\mathcal{o}\left(\varepsilon^{-4}\right)$ ) under the strongly convex-nonconcave (resp., convex-nonconcave) setting. to the best of our knowledge, these theoretical performance guarantees under this setting have not been obtained before in the literature.

furthermore, we consider more general nonsmooth multi-block nonconvex-(strongly) concave and (strongly) convex-nonconcave minimax problems, which include smooth one block setting as a special case. we propose a block alternating proximal gradient algorithm (bapg) to solve it. at each iteration, only simple proximal gradient steps are employed for updating one block variable, and for each block of multi-block variables alternatively. we prove the gradient complexity of the proposed bapg algorithm under four different settings. to the best of our knowledge, these are the state-of-the-art single loop algorithms under nonconvex-concave setting and the first two theoretically guaranteed convergence results reported in the literature under (non)smooth multi-blocks (strongly) convex-nonconcave minimax problems. our development shows that single loop algorithms do not need to solve the inner subproblem, and thus can be easily generalized. this is also the reason that the iteration complexity analysis will be more difficult than that of nested-looped algorithms. it will also be interesting to study whether the iteration complexity of the proposed agp algorithm is tightest or not among single loop algorithms.
